Abstract: When a storm can head north or south, a forecast down the middle may score better under squared error than either plausible path. The same paradox appears when video generators are tested as world models: fewer denoising steps can raise physics scores even as videos blur or stop moving early. Why? Physics-IQ compares each generated video with one recording, although a scene can have multiple valid futures. An exact decomposition shows its scores can reward reduced variation among generated futures, even when their distribution becomes less accurate. We introduce Kernel Physics-IQ to remove this incentive in expectation, needing only two independent samples per condition. Two-step sampling lowers Kernel Physics-IQ in all five image-to-video models. For CogVideoX, the official score rises by 0.055 while ours falls by 0.131. Changing the sampling schedule reveals further differences official scores miss. In controlled physical systems with known outcome probabilities, our metric favors the true distribution over collapsed predictions. In short, a world model should be judged not only by how closely one video matches what happened, but by how faithfully its predictions represent what could have happened.

</details>

Abstract: Image color enhancement aims to improve visual appeal, but previous methods often learn to reproduce ground-truth expert targets. These targets reflect the preferences of a few experts and cover a limited range of aesthetic choices. Matching them does not ensure stronger aesthetic quality across different tastes and scenes. In this paper, we introduce AesDistill, a framework for distilling generative aesthetics into image color enhancement. We use 4 leading open-source large generative models to produce 64,644 edited images as teacher targets at a cost of about 3,360 H100 GPU-hours, forming the AesDistill Dataset. AesDistill also includes AesDistill Student, a 1.13M-parameter model that learns teacher color changes through confidence-weighted supervision while preserving the source image's semantic structure. We evaluate AesDistill with 12 aesthetic and 4 image-quality assessment models. Every teacher's targets rank above the FiveK and PPR10K expert target means in the joint assessment. Across both datasets, AesDistill Student outperforms all 20 baseline methods and ranks directly behind the 4 teachers. These results demonstrate that distilling aesthetics from generative models enables a compact student to outperform methods trained on expert targets.

Abstract: An image signal processor does more than reconstruct an image. Most neural ISPs learn a single fixed transformation and apply it to every scene. We instead make the adaptation an explicit part of the network. Our model, LG FiLM, keeps one shared encoder decoder and places a small mixture of affine experts at its bottleneck; the experts scales, shifts, and mixing weights are predicted from local neighborhood evidence together with a global scene descriptor. Reconstruction stays in a single shared pathway and only the processing mode is switched. Abstract: Given an image and successive user instructions, sequential image editing should return an updated image that satisfies each new request and preserves earlier requirements that remain active. This lets users develop a result without repeatedly restating or repairing their decisions. The difficulty is that a retrieved image can contain a valid color on an obsolete surface, while the current image may lack the newly requested material. Neither reference selection nor a precise object mask alone specifies how to reconcile attributes occupying the same region. We present TRACE, which separates the continuing attribute from its visual carrier and prioritizes local appearance operations under complete-request checks. Active-goal binding identifies what should persist, carrier selection supplies suitable current or newly generated pixels, and tonal calibration reduces tinting near highlights and shadows. Experiments across frozen editors, independent visual readers, new source images, and complete sequential benchmarks support joint color-material recovery and show how local execution preserves the surrounding scene.

Abstract: Multimodal sarcasm detection requires textual, visual, and contextual evidence, particularly when multiple images provide complementary cues. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate auxiliary information from a post's text and images, but their outputs may mix useful background knowledge with redundant visual descriptions. We propose SEAF, a dual-branch framework for selective semantic enrichment and visual evidence fusion. SEAF assigns generated fields to distinct roles: entity background enriches semantic input, while image descriptions supply alignment statistics. The semantic branch combines the original post text, background from a frozen MLLM, and optical character recognition (OCR) text. The aligned visual branch combines global and local visual features with statistics of cross-modal and inter-image similarity. Text-Anchored Smoothed Aggregation (TASA) weights images by their similarity to the original post text while preserving contributions from images with low literal similarity. Summing the branch classification scores integrates contextual semantics with visual evidence. Experiments on the multi-image MMSD3.0 benchmark show that SEAF outperforms all compared methods.

Abstract: Given past images and known actions, visual continuation generates future frames from one evolving scene state. Keeping this state revisable matters when an object reappears or a later view changes an earlier interpretation. Once past images cannot be replayed, a summary sufficient for current rendering can lose candidates needed after correction. We establish matching sparse and dense bounds on the bits needed for later correction under stable observations and bounded disagreement. For static streams at the stated smoothness and margin scales, correction incurs at least logarithmic overhead in one dimension and polynomial overhead in higher dimensions. Coherent Archive Memory (CAM) stores geometric summaries with exact updates under clipped expansion and coordinate assignments, then uses historical existence constraints to select one state for all requested frames. Exhaustive photographic tests show strict worst-truth image-risk gains from joint witnesses, while controlled neural continuations recover positions hidden in the current view.

Abstract: We propose VC-VQA, a large-scale benchmark for vision-centric knowledge reasoning, aiming to systematically evaluate the capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition and complex multi-stage reasoning. Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily treat visual information as entity recognition and rely on text-based external knowledge, VC-VQA explicitly models the role of visual information by considering both its source (internal vs. external images) and stage of involvement (pre-retrieval vs. post-retrieval). Specifically, VC-VQA requires models to retrieve external visual evidence, identify visual clues before retrieval, and localize, verify, or match retrieved knowledge against the internal image. We further construct a controlled multimodal knowledge base with traceable textual and visual evidence for each sample. We evaluate 21 representative MLLMs under four settings: no external knowledge, with provided evidence, knowledge base retrieval, and open-web search. Experimental results lead to three key findings: current models still struggle with vision-centric knowledge reasoning; fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition remains the primary bottleneck; and agentic retrieval highlights the value of multi-step planning and cross-modal knowledge integration. Overall, VC-VQA provides a more challenging benchmark for evaluating vision-centric knowledge reasoning in MLLMs. Our code and dataset are available at https://github.com/CASIA-IVA-Lab/VC-VQA.

Abstract: Diffusion Transformers generate high-quality images and videos, but every sampling step requires a full network evaluation. Training-free caching reduces this cost by reusing or forecasting network outputs across steps, but common approaches use per-model calibrated skip criteria or maintain per-layer prediction histories. We present D3-Cache, which applies Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD) to Diffusion sampling: it fits DMD to a short history of exact velocities and forecasts only the velocity that the scheduler consumes. A horizon-aware gate decides when to skip: whenever the network is evaluated, its output is compared with the forecast for that step, and the larger the error, the sooner the network is evaluated again, up to a cap on consecutive skipped steps. D3-Cache requires no training or per-model offline calibration curve and adds negligible memory. It shares one history length and risk tolerance across the four evaluated models. On four state-of-the-art image and video models, D3-Cache achieves speedups of 2.6-3.8x with high fidelity to the base model output.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal image fusion traditionally generates a single fused image from paired observations at a specific viewpoint, limiting comprehensive scene perception and adaptability to diverse perceptual objectives. To overcome these limitations, we present LIFT, a novel framework for our proposed language-controlled 3D image fusion (L3D-IF) paradigm that extends multimodal image fusion from 2D images to free-viewpoint 3D scenes, enabling language-guided fusion tailored to diverse perceptual objectives. First, LIFT formulates Semantic Modality Binding (SMB), which interprets language instructions to identify semantic targets and their associated modality preferences and grounds these targets in corresponding regions of a shared Gaussian scene, establishing persistent semantic-to-spatial correspondences for consistent fusion control across viewpoints. Second, Language-Conditioned Gaussian Appearance (LCGA) is developed to achieve scene-wide multimodal appearance modeling by combining adaptive multimodal blending with language- and viewpoint-conditioned appearance refinement, enabling spatially adaptive realization of diverse fusion appearances while preserving source fidelity at pure-modality endpoints. Finally, we construct a Multi-View Language-Supervision (MVLS) dataset that associates language instructions and calibrated viewpoints with modulated fused images, providing effective supervision for learning instruction-dependent fusion from finite multi-view observations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LIFT achieves coherent multimodal fusion across continuous viewpoints, accurate semantic target localization, and consistent regional fusion control under diverse language instructions.

</details>

Abstract: Image-to-slide reconstruction must recover both visual appearance and native object structure for subsequent editing. Yet visually faithful charts and tables may lack editable series and cells, and direct code generation couples content prediction to rendering implementation. We present Slide2PPT, a model-agnostic, training-free framework that equips both open- and closed-source vision-language models with native PowerPoint reconstruction capabilities. Through a unified typed interface, the model predicts object records while a fixed renderer materializes their native properties, enabling integration with different backbones without task-specific training or architectural changes. Localization-guided refinement then connects visual discrepancies to object records for correction. Regional updates preserve unselected records, while whole-slide updates address cross-region relationships. Record refinement and targeted object editing support structural and attribute-level changes, with rendered feedback governing iterative acceptance. To test whether later features can also correct errors, we degrade both entries and restore only the later ones. The same blur costs both Qwen models 50 percentage points of accuracy on stripe orientation. The 32B model recovers 48 points and the 8B model only 8. In split inference on these questions, DeepStack can then send only its main-entry features at higher precision. This takes 49% fewer bytes than all entries at a shared precision, at similar accuracy.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) predict future videos and actions with one backbone, and their imagined future is often seen as a safety signal. We introduce SteerTrace, a targeted universal attack on WAMs that also traces its effect inside the model. One $\ell_\infty$-bounded (4/255) perturbation per model, shared across frames, tasks, and camera views, pushes the executed action toward an attacker-chosen target. On eight WAMs from four families (FastWAM, Faster-WAM, OpenWAM and LingBot-VA) on LIBERO and RoboTwin, it drives task success close to zero and moves the attacked action toward the target. Same-budget noise and BadWAM produce little action shift in the target direction. We use the signed targeted effect to measure how much of the effect each intervention removes or reproduces. Under the standard attack, restoring future tokens removes 61-94% of the targeted effect in the five models whose action experts attend to them. When the attack is constrained to keep the predicted future close to the clean prediction, the targeted effect can become more dependent on current-frame tokens. In three of these five models, restoring current-frame tokens removes 87-99% of the effect, compared with 9-15% for future tokens. In six models, restoring just 0.36% of the FFN neurons in layers 10-29 of the video expert removes 72-96% of the targeted effect. Residual directions 2-16 hold only 11-18% of the shift energy, yet removing them cancels most of the targeted effect.

Abstract: 3D visual grounding (3D-VG) is a task that bridges the natural language and 3D space, serving as a foundational capability for embodied intelligence and human-computer interaction. Although recent 3D-VG agents overcome the reliance on pre-processed 3D point clouds, they essentially employ a unidirectional, pipeline-style tool-calling architecture. This architecture separates semantic understanding from geometric reconstruction, leading to error accumulation and an inability to autonomously recover from failures. To address this flaw, this paper proposes the Dual-Anchor Collaborative Agent (DACA), which reinterprets 3D-VG as a semantic-geometric dual-anchor collaborative reasoning paradigm. DACA comprises two complementary specialized tools: the semantic anchor tool holds the visual semantic features of the target and achieves efficient target tracking via optical flow and VLM local verification; the geometric anchor tool holds the 3D spatial geometric features of the target and performs global spatial reasoning via voxelization and multi-keypoint projection. The two components exchange information bidirectionally via a standardized tool call protocol, autonomously deciding on the next perception-action step to form a closed-loop optimization. Experiments on the ScanRefer and Nr3D benchmarks demonstrate that zero-shot DACA achieves an overall Acc@0.25 of 73.6\% on ScanRefer and 70.4\% overall accuracy on Nr3D, far surpassing the latest zero-shot 3D-VG methods. The code and models are available at https://github.com/liujia99/DACA

Abstract: Can we reveal what is inside an opaque box by observing its surface vibrations? Objects inside a box alter its mechanical response, providing cues about their hidden locations. We introduce a method that uses these cues to predict a box's 2D occupancy layout entirely remotely. A nearby speaker excites vibrations, which we measure on one visible wall using a grid of laser points and a camera measuring each point's speckle-pattern shifts. Our model, WhisperBox, learns to encode these vibrations into a latent representation and decode it into an occupancy map of the box's contents. The method requires no physical contact to excite or sense vibrations. We demonstrate layout recovery across various boxes and single- and multiple-object scenes, with generalization to unseen object instances, positions, orientations, and weights, as well as changes in laser placement and excitation source. Our analysis reveals that informative frequency bands need not coincide with the box's resonances and that the learned latent space captures object placement and supports arithmetic operations. These results demonstrate the potential of remote vibration sensing to infer spatial layouts hidden from view.

Abstract: Skeleton graph attention for autism video analysis weights joints by content alone, giving an erratic wrist the same standing as a still one. Stereotypy, however, is defined by how unpredictably movement unfolds rather than how much of it occurs: in our cohort, every amplitude-based kinematic descriptor falls in stereotyped clips, while windowed Sample Entropy of angular velocity alone rises (Hedges' g = +1.05). We compute this irregularity as a per-joint, per-frame tensor that structurally drives computation. The proposed chaos-guided graph attention and mixture-of-experts model (CGM) couples two mechanisms. First, a learned pairwise term added to the graph-attention logit concentrates attention on irregular joints and re-ranks the remaining edge weight toward jointly irregular pairs; its unconstrained coefficient neither vanishes nor inverts, and zeroing the tensor realigns attention in 94% of clips. Second, a gate conditioned on the same signal routes each frame to two of four experts, whose weights track frame-level irregularity. Under subject-grouped five-fold cross-validation on 116 clips, CGM reaches 0.930 balanced accuracy, exceeding the graph-attention baseline by +0.187; the component ordering replicates on the public MMASD dataset, and on held-out Panoptic Studio sessions, CGM keeps 0.86 specificity without retraining, against at most 0.35 for chaos-free baselines. In ablation, routing without irregularity yields no reliable gain, whereas chaos modulation alone contributes +0.132. A severity index from the same tensor reaches 0.87 split-half reliability, separates the cohorts with Hedges' g = +1.08, and stays consistent within children across therapy recordings, supporting video-only monitoring of stereotypy during intervention.

</details>

Abstract: Professional video workflows operate at qualities that learned video codecs do not reach. We measure how much of a professional quality range a codec covers, from the lowest quality of the standard test conditions, VVC at QP 37, to the highest 4:2:2 grade of the production codec ProRes. To compare inter-frame coding alone, every codec receives original frames as references at the intra positions. On 10-bit YCbCr 4:2:2 test sequences, four recent learned codecs cover 24 to 38% of this range. Their encoders are feed-forward networks with a fixed latent capacity, the number of latent values per sample, of at most 0.25, and removing their quantization raises the quality by at most 1 dB. We propose a learned inter-frame codec whose encoder searches, as the encoders of conventional codecs do. It searches block motion, which a learned network refines, and it searches among learned block transforms whose latent capacity ranges up to 1, selecting the partition, the skip decisions, and the quantized coefficients by a rate-distortion cost. Our codec reaches the upper end of the range on all six sequences. With all residual blocks fixed to 16x16, whose latent capacity is 0.25, the same codec covers 55% of the range, with or without quantization.

</details>

Abstract: Promptable segmenters answer what a thing is. Robots, surveillance systems and image editors build on their answers. We study promptable concept maps (PCM), which extend SAM 3's promptable concept segmentation from noun instances to maps of concepts named by short noun phrases such as `"transparent object", with "none" as an answer. PCM-Bench groups the standard test sets of seven such tasks by the question they ask. On it, we find that SAM 3 sees more than it detects. Its semantic path responds to many of these concepts, yet a low image-level presence score can block every detection of its instance path. We present UNVEIL, which trains a copy of SAM 3's semantic path for these maps, with LoRA on copies of its last blocks. A separate presence decoder learns from verified negatives whether a concept occurs. With one text phrase per query, UNVEIL maps region concepts such as salient and camouflaged objects, shadows, transparent objects and mirrors. The same weights map thin structures such as edges and skeletons as well as object categories. Run next to SAM 3, it leaves SAM 3's own outputs unchanged. Abstract: Identifying a driver's gaze target provides object-level information for visual attention monitoring in intelligent vehicles. Gaze-zone classification and continuous attention localization provide limited information about the identity of the attended scene target. This paper proposes a modular framework that identifies object- or region-level gaze targets from synchronized in-cabin driver images and forward-view road images. The framework comprises four components: MFGaze, which combines a Vision Transformer pretrained with a masked autoencoder (MAE) and head-pose conditioning through feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM); panoptic scene understanding, which constructs a structured candidate set; polynomial ridge regression, which maps gaze features to 2D scene-image coordinates through individualized calibration; and entropy-driven target inference, which adaptively fuses boundary-distance geometry with class-level semantic priors. Experiments evaluate the framework using three public gaze datasets, a laboratory test using Cityscapes road scenes and self-collected gaze data, and the Look Both Ways (LBW) dataset. The complete framework achieves an overall Top-1 gaze target identification accuracy of 96.27% in the laboratory experiment. After sequence-specific calibration, it achieves Top-1 accuracies of 79.92% for task-relevant objects and 85.58% for the road class on LBW. MFGaze achieves the lowest mean angular errors among the compared methods, with 4.06$^\circ$, 4.23$^\circ$, and 10.52$^\circ$ on MPIIFaceGaze, ETH-XGaze, and Gaze360, respectively, and demonstrates strong cross-dataset generalization. Sensitivity analyses show stable performance under hyperparameter perturbations. These results support the framework as a source of object-level gaze information for driver monitoring.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is the stage in which Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) advance their capability to reason over video. However, the VideoLLM also learns to answer from textual regularities of its training data, a shortcut that bypasses the video. The regularities behind the shortcut are hard to define at the data level, and the shortcut forms regardless of training epoch, data size, or backbone. The shortcut is hidden behind a rise in accuracy within the training distribution, yet it is not required for the rise and even costs SFT the gain the video offers under distribution shift. We therefore propose Video-Evidence Training via Orthogonal projection (VETO), which intervenes on the fine-tuning update where the shortcut is written rather than on the data. VETO calibrates the subspace that the text alone occupies in the activations of the base model and projects the update onto its orthogonal complement. The VideoLLM is thus left to ground its answer in the full input, with the shortcut suppressed as it forms and never defined. Across seven VideoLLMs, VETO preserves the gain of SFT on the training distribution and suppresses the shortcut. On six benchmarks under shift it gains more than SFT and answers from the full input rather than the shortcut. Code is available at https://github.com/Youngwoo-git/VETO.

</details>

Abstract: We present FAB (Fast and Accurate Body estimation), a human body estimator that jointly improves inference efficiency and body-recovery fidelity. Rather than predicting only parametric mesh-model parameters, FAB directly predicts human skeleton joints and dense surface points. In images, it predicts dense 2D and 3D body points alongside a parametric Momentum Human Rig (MHR) head, then fits SMPL in one learned pass followed by two Gauss-Newton steps. Its ViT-L student, FAB-L, distilled from a fine-tuned SAM 3D Body, runs in real time for one person on an RTX 4070 Ti SUPER, from detection to a fitted body, at 4.6x the speed of SAM 3D Body's eager-PyTorch body-only path, while improving accuracy on most camera-space benchmarks. The same network predicts heel, toe, hand, and knee contacts, which connect the body to the scene. FAB also estimates world-space motion, fuses static-scene depth around the visible person, and aligns body and scene using predicted contacts. Abstract: Existing 3D generation methods synthesize visually compelling scenes from fixed inputs, yet struggle to continually expand and revise a 3D world as new observations arrive. Iterative approaches often fuse real observations and generated content into a single representation, treating generated content as established structure rather than revisable predictions. Consequently, early generation errors can persist and accumulate despite new evidence. We propose World-delta, an incremental 3D scene generation framework combining uncertainty-aware persistent world modeling with delta-guided diffusion for continual scene expansion and correction. Our framework comprises three components: 1) Visual Evidence Encoding integrates geometric and semantic cues from incoming images into unified world tokens. 2) Observation-Calibrated World Modeling maintains a persistent latent world state that distinguishes observation-supported content from provisional completion hypotheses. New evidence drives selective world deltas to revise conflicting hypotheses while preserving well-supported content. 3) Incremental World Diffusion Generation uses delta-derived masks to restrict diffusion to regions requiring updates, freezing unchanged regions. Only affected 3D chunks are decoded and integrated into the existing scene. Together, these components enable continual world expansion and revision while reducing redundant generation and preserving scene consistency. Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of World-delta in incremental 3D scene generation, observation-driven correction, scene consistency, and update efficiency over successive scene updates.

</details>

Abstract: Medical vision-language agents increasingly rely on segmentation tools, and recent work has focused on acquiring the right mask, often treating a good mask as the end point of tool use. But do these agents use the resulting evidence effectively? To answer this question, we need a benchmark that separates evidence use from segmentation quality, spans multiple medical image types, and allows the same mask to be presented in different ways. Existing medical VQA datasets do not jointly provide these properties. We therefore construct Mask-Evidence MedVQA, a 2.5K-example benchmark spanning four image types, where each question is paired with an answer-relevant ground-truth mask and multiple possible renderings. Our study reveals two limitations of direct tool use. First, evidence presentation matters. Different renderings of the same fixed mask can substantially change model behavior. Second, masks introduce an evidence trade-off. Highlighting a relevant region can expose diagnostic evidence the model previously missed, but the added visual cue can also induce incorrect associations. As a result, even a ground-truth mask can correct some answers while turning initially correct ones into incorrect ones. Together, these findings show that acquiring the right mask is not enough. An agent must also decide how to present the evidence and whether it should change the answer. We therefore introduce TUNE (Tool Use is Not Enough), an evidence-management harness that explicitly manages what evidence to acquire, how to present it, and whether to accept the resulting answer update. Across three public medical VQA benchmarks, evidence management improves the fine-tuned Qwen3.5-9B backbone's mean accuracy by 4.0 percentage points. Full TUNE improves over direct tool use by 9.1 points on average across seven benchmarks and achieves competitive results against existing medical VLMs and agents.

</details>

Abstract: Flow matching enables high-quality image and video generation, but its iterative sampling process requires repeated evaluations of large neural networks. Existing few-step methods can distort the output and reduce its agreement with a many-step reference. We propose TrajCal, a training-free calibration framework for few-step sampling. Based on scatter search, a well-established metaheuristic for difficult optimization problems, TrajCal starts with diverse seed schedules, deforms and combines these candidates, and selects one fixed schedule by final output fidelity for reuse on new inputs. For both image and video generation, TrajCal achieves higher reference fidelity than all compared baselines at low evaluation budgets while maintaining competitive visual quality. The search is effective even with randomly generated seeds, while trajectory-based seeds further improve fidelity. For cross-style generalization, photography-calibrated schedules also improve fidelity on unseen image styles.

Abstract: Recalling where and when an object was last seen and who saw it requires recognizing it across days and people. Existing long-video benchmarks focus on events, object categories, or relative locations, leaving instance-level spatial memory largely untested in multimodal models. To address this gap, we present EgoWitness, a benchmark for instance-level spatial memory and reasoning in egocentric video. Its 8,453 human-verified questions span approximately 324 hours of week-long and short-horizon recordings and test seven abilities, from recognizing an object or room to recalling who saw it and reasoning about room visits. Over one million reviewed masks of 34 registered landmarks and 21 movable-object labels are linked through room timelines. Among twelve direct vision-language models (VLMs), open-weight models reach at most 46.97% accuracy, and none exceeds 35% on identifying who saw an object last. We therefore propose EgoClue, a spatial-memory agent that retrieves candidate intervals, verifies instance identity, and computes spatial and temporal relations with five tools. Abstract: Recognizing when a requested spatial condition holds in streaming video can require maintaining and updating spatial knowledge across changing viewpoints. We study this capability through proactive spatial reasoning, where a model receives a query in advance and determines whether and when to respond as the stream unfolds. Experiments reveal a substantial gap between VLMs and humans, while ProSpace-VLM improves timely responses and correct abstention over fine-tuned baselines. Abstract: Accurately extracting and mapping road surface elements is crucial for applications such as high-definition maps, road asset management, and intelligent transportation systems. This study first provides a comprehensive review of the benchmark datasets for extracting and mapping road elements, encompassing overhead-to-perspective data, large-to-local scales, and coarse-to-fine details. Subsequently, we thoroughly review the relevant approaches for extracting road surface elements. However, we argue that existing datasets still require enhancements in semantic richness, data modality diversity, and scenario complexity. To bridge these gaps, this paper presents WHU-Road3D, a large-scale multimodal dataset for fine-grained road surface mapping to address these shortcomings. WHU-Road3D provides approximately 100 km of diverse Chinese highways and urban streets. It features a rich combination of MLS point clouds, associated panoramic images, vehicle trajectories, detailed vectorized annotations, and pointwise instance annotations for road surface elements. The dataset carefully annotates 15 distinct categories of road elements, including common markings like road boundaries, zebra crossings, lane dividers, stop lines, as well as 10 types of direction symbols. To our knowledge, WHU-Road3D stands as the first multimodal dataset for mapping such a comprehensive range of road surface elements, designed to facilitate cutting-edge research in instance segmentation, full-element extraction and mapping on road surfaces. Beyond these immediate applications, WHU-Road3D also provides a valuable source for hierarchical road topology reasoning, map updating, and street scene generation. Abstract: Although memory-augmented architectures have expanded the ability of large vision-language models (LVLMs) to process long videos, existing retrieval processes still largely rely on global semantic matching and lack state-driven temporal control, often returning fragmented or redundant evidence and failing to maintain a coherent evidence chain. To address this challenge, we propose EvoMem, a time-aware long-video memory agent guided by self-evolving retrieval skills. EvoMem constructs a hierarchical time-indexed memory, where different types of evidence are aligned onto a unified video timeline; during retrieval, temporal cues from acquired evidence are further converted into explicit signals that constrain subsequent memory access. To address recurring procedural errors in long-video question answering, such as unclear retrieval targets, improper memory access paths, or premature stopping, EvoMem introduces retrieval skills to provide process-level guidance, enabling the agent to dynamically adjust retrieval content, memory operations, and target temporal ranges according to the current evidence state, while deciding when to stop. Furthermore, inspired by reinforcement learning, we formulate skill evolution as a reward-guided policy improvement process. Each candidate skill is treated as an external policy patch, and rewards are assigned according to answer-correctness state transitions on the validation set. In this way, effective skills are accepted and low-quality skills are rejected without updating model parameters. Experiments on four long-video question answering benchmarks show that EvoMem significantly outperforms existing baselines, achieving an average accuracy of 60.1% and an average improvement of 8.6 percentage points over the previous best methods, demonstrating its effectiveness for long-video reasoning.

Abstract: Vision Language Models (VLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities in qualitative spatial reasoning, but remain limited in quantitative spatial reasoning. Current work leverages the VLM agent to invoke external vision tools to perform computation. However, these methods usually bind each functional requirement to a single predefined vision specialist, implicitly resulting in fixed and rigid tool pipelines. In practice, addressing diverse spatial scenarios and tasks demands a broader diversity in pipeline orchestration and vision specialist selection to achieve robust spatial reasoning. However, such diversity leads to a combinatorial execution space, making it challenging for the agent to orchestrate the optimal pipelines and specialists for different scenarios.
  In this work, we propose SpatialMem, a memory framework that enables a VLM agent to accumulate tool-use experience and leverage that memory to orchestrate vision specialists to solve the spatial reasoning problem. SpatialMem builds a two-level memory structure, with task memory capturing tool orchestration and scene memory capturing tool selection. It further introduces a dual-state trajectory optimization process to preserve successful tool experiences and refine suboptimal ones. Moreover, existing spatial reasoning benchmarks suffer from quality issues and limited coverage of scene types and tasks. To address these gap, we propose MetricBench, a benchmark covering both indoor and outdoor scenes and three quantitative spatial reasoning tasks. Our method achieves state-of-the-art results on MetricBench, Q-Spatial, and SpatilRGPT-Bench, outperforming best VLM baselines by 32% on MetricBench. Additionally, the memory design yields a 9.1% absolute gain over a memory-free agent on MetricBench.

Abstract: Flat color-block drafts specify layout and regional color while leaving interior detail unspecified, yet pretrained flow-based generators can preserve their flat appearance even under photorealistic prompts. We trace this behavior to a cross-scale mechanism linking fine pixel variation to regional means in variational autoencoder (VAE) latent space. Flattening photo regions systematically shifts their latent means, with offsets concentrated along a shared channel direction within each backbone. Controlled field exchanges in FLUX.1-dev and Qwen-Image establish the effect of this displacement on texture generation: adding the photo-derived regional mean field to a flat latent restores texture, whereas adding its complementary field alone largely preserves flatness. A second-order encoding expansion explains how encoder curvature can convert zero-mean perturbations into mean shifts. An analytically solvable conditional Gaussian flow shows how regional noise averaging enables near-unit endpoint sensitivity at high signal-to-noise ratios. This mechanism motivates training-free rendering through pixel dither before encoding, mean-field scaling, and early boundary holding. Paired low-rank adaptation (LoRA) with draft conditioning improves content recovery and produces photo-like regional means under the standard velocity objective. Explicit regional mean guidance remains effective after adaptation, continuously controlling texture and improving color fidelity. These findings connect encoding shifts to persistent generation behavior and support interventions evaluated for texture, spatial fidelity, and semantic recovery.

Abstract: Facial deepfake detectors are usually trained on blending fakes, where a generated face is pasted back into a real frame. Recent generators synthesize the entire face and leave no traces of blending. On a benchmark of 19 datasets released from 2019 to 2026, the best prior facial deepfake detector reaches a mean AUROC of 96.2% on ten blending datasets, but none exceeds 78.1% on five datasets of entire face synthesis (EFS). AI-generated content (AIGC) detectors do not close this gap. We propose TwinD, a detector trained on FaceForensics++ fakes and two kinds of twins: fakes made from a real frame. A reconstruction twin is a real frame reconstructed by an autoencoder, with the real pixels put back in a random region. It targets the neural traces of a decoder. A blob twin blends an altered copy of a real frame with the frame itself, targeting blending artifacts. It needs no generator and no face-shaped mask. Together, these twins provide two complementary forensic cues that generalize to unseen manipulations. TwinDreaches a mean AUROC of 96.3%, 8.9 points above the prior state-of-the-art, with 98.1% on the blending datasets and 95.6% on the EFS datasets. Trained on twins alone, without a deepfake dataset, single models still reach 94.3% on average over three seeds. Abstract: Robotic world models predict future video frames from the current observation and a sequence of robot actions. While recent models follow the given actions closely, manipulated objects often collapse or move inconsistently with the action. We attribute this to the conditioning: an action, or a rendering of the robot, specifies where the robot goes but not how the scene responds, leaving a single video model to infer the interaction dynamics implicitly while also rendering appearance. We therefore separate dynamics prediction from visual rendering, with 3D point dynamics as the proxy between them. Specifically, we lift the first frame into 3D points, simulate how they respond to the robot's motion with a learned point dynamics model, and render the simulated points back into pixel-aligned videos that any video model can read. In controlled experiments on the DROID dataset with two video renderers of different architectures, this point dynamics proxy reduces object motion error by 8.6% and 6.2% without degrading image quality, and by 8.1% on a real bimanual robot. Moreover, the video follows the simulation: more accurate dynamics yield more accurate object motion, and editing the points steers the video. This suggests that advances in dynamics modeling can translate into more faithful robotic world models.

</details>

We introduce an egocentric stereo and RGB-D benchmark covering interactions with objects of varying stiffness. Real-world experiments demonstrate improved reconstruction accuracy and up to 14.4% lower future tracking error than PhysTwin at interactive speeds.

Abstract: Editing a 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) capture of a person so that they wear a new garment requires every rendered view to agree on the same garment details. Existing 3D virtual try-on (VTON) pipelines, however, edit training views largely independently and fuse them with hand-designed heuristics such as manually chosen reference views and fixed attention-blending weights. The resulting inconsistencies are baked into the 3D representation and surface as texture drift, blurred prints, and implausible geometry in novel views. We present GS-VTON-MVT, a three-stage framework that combines implicit and explicit 3D reasoning for consistent 3DGS try-on. (i) A multi-view inpainting transformer jointly edits a set of training views with full cross-view self-attention, so that cross-view correspondence is learned rather than imposed. (ii) A geometry-guided multi-modal ControlNet conditions the diffusion editor on the garment image together with depth and surface normals rendered from the current 3DGS state, supplying explicit structure where visual evidence is weakest. (iii) A pose-adaptive persona-aware attention replaces the fixed blending weight between current-view and reference-view features with weights derived from camera-pose proximity. On the 3D-VTONBench benchmark, GS-VTON-MVT improves FID, KID, LPIPS-based cross-view consistency, and user preference over general-purpose 3D editors and prior 3D try-on methods.

Abstract: We present PaveSplat, a feed-forward Gaussian splatting method that adds a learned residual to the analytic surface-aligned covariance, keeping surfels paving the visible surface across rendering resolutions. Pixel-aligned feed-forward Gaussian splatting predicts one primitive per pixel from a few images in a single pass, and its rendering quality depends on how well these primitives cover the surface. Recent surfel-based methods compute the surface-aligned covariance analytically from predicted depth. However, this covariance has no way to correct the surfel orientation where the depth-derived frame is ill-posed. The surfels then cover the surface loosely; at the training resolution each surfel's one-pixel minimum footprint covers the gaps, but they show up as holes when the same prediction is re-rendered at a higher resolution. To address this, we keep the analytic covariance as an initialization, with its axes aligned to the surface tangents, and add an identity-initialized unit-quaternion residual that reorients a surfel only where this improves rendering, turning surfels that the analytic frame mis-aims back onto the surface. On RealEstate10K, PaveSplat achieves the best PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS among single-Gaussian-per-pixel methods at every scale (1x-8x). The native-resolution gain is small; the benefit appears when the same prediction is rendered at higher display resolutions without re-running the network. On zero-shot DL3DV, it leads PSNR within this group at every scale and ranks first or second on SSIM/LPIPS. A coverage-hole analysis shows fewer uncovered pixels than both baselines under high-resolution re-rendering.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous rendezvous and proximity operations require not only an accurate estimate of the pose of the target spacecraft, but also a reliable indication of when this estimate can be trusted. Conformal prediction provides such an indication with a formal guarantee, by producing pose uncertainty sets that contain the true pose with a specified probability. Such a guarantee, however, assumes that calibration and test data follow the same distribution, an assumption that is violated in orbital perception, where labelled calibration images are available only from synthetic renderings while operational images are real. In this work, we show on the SPEED+ dataset that conformal pose estimation calibrated on synthetic images loses its coverage guarantee on real images, and that many of the resulting failures are silent: the predicted pose set appears valid but excludes the true pose. To address this, we calibrate on synthetic images translated to the appearance of real images. Since translation alters appearance while preserving geometry, the synthetic pose labels remain valid and no labelled real images are required. Experiments with two translation models show that this calibration restores coverage above the specified level for miscoverage levels of 0.1 and above, and reduces silent failures from up to 43.5% to at most 3.3% of the test images, bringing conformal pose estimation a step closer to trustworthy deployment in space.

</details>

Abstract: Recovering metric-scale geometry from observations at a single viewpoint remains fundamentally ill-posed, limiting the reliability of novel view synthesis. Recent methods rely on learned monocular depth priors, which provide strong structural estimates but can suffer from scale ambiguity and dataset-dependent biases. We propose DfD-GS, a feed-forward framework that predicts a metric-scale 3D Gaussian scene representation from a single-view all-in-focus--defocused image pair. The key idea is to exploit defocus blur as a physical metric depth cue. DfD-GS first estimates a defocus-based metric disparity map from the all-in-focus--defocused image pair and the corresponding defocus strength. This estimate is used to align and refine a monocular depth prior to metric scale. Visible and hidden 3D Gaussians are then predicted for occlusion-aware novel view synthesis. Experiments on synthetic-defocus datasets constructed from Booster and Middlebury show that DfD-GS outperforms single-image baselines in metric depth estimation and novel view synthesis. On WildRGB-D, DfD-GS achieves lower AbsRel and LPIPS but higher depth RMSE than the single-image baselines. Additional analyses show that DfD-GS maintains a more stable metric scale under field-of-view changes. Qualitative results on a real dataset show recovery of the main scene structure under optical defocus.

Abstract: Text-to-video generation has made remarkable progress with diffusion models and transformer-based video architectures, yet generating coherent long videos from evolving textual descriptions remains challenging. Training long-video models from scratch requires substantial computation and large-scale multi-event data, while training-free multi-prompt generation must activate new prompt semantics without breaking temporal continuity. We identify a frequency-agnostic boundary coupling problem in existing transition strategies: weak coupling causes abrupt changes, flicker, and background jumps, whereas overly strong or coarse coupling may over-preserve source semantics and produce ghosting or duplicated subjects around prompt boundaries. To address this problem, we propose FreqSync, a training-free framework that formulates prompt transitions as frequency-selective boundary coupling. FreqSync combines Frequency-Synchronized Source-Conditioned Attention (FS-SCA) with Residual-Clipped Spectral Stitching (RCSS), enabling adjacent segments to share stable low- and mid-frequency structure and motion cues while keeping high-frequency prompt-specific details target-dominant under explicit residual constraints. Extensive experiments show that FreqSync achieves state-of-the-art transition smoothness and prompt alignment while maintaining strong perceptual video quality, demonstrating its effectiveness for coherent multi-prompt long video generation.

Abstract: Unified multimodal models aim to support visual understanding and generation within a single framework. However, prevailing approaches often separate their visual representations or backbone computation to accommodate the different requirements of these two tasks, which limits the degree of unification. In this paper, we present HiUni, which revisits this design choice and explores whether a pretrained vision language model can be directly extended into a unified model with extensive parameter sharing. Specifically, HiUni reuses the same language-aligned ViT and dense Transformer backbone for both capabilities, while confining task-specific computation to the output side. Yet such a design introduces two key challenges. Generation in pixel space is difficult to optimize, and semantic abstraction in pretrained ViT discards fine spatial details required for synthesis. To address these challenges, HiUni introduces a \emph{Pixel Diffusion Head} tailored to direct pixel-space optimization and a lightweight \emph{HiGate} pathway that provides fine-grained spatial details near the output stage. Extensive experiments show that HiUni achieves a similar average understanding score to the pretrained VLM while attaining competitive performance on image generation and editing. Controlled comparisons and analyses further demonstrate that a single dense backbone can effectively support both capabilities without task-specific backbone computation, highlighting a simpler path toward unified multimodal modeling.

Abstract: The proliferation of AI-generated videos has raised growing concerns about misinformation, fraud, and potential societal risks, creating an urgent need for detection. Reconstruction-error-based methods have shown strong empirical performance for AI-generated image detection by exploiting discrepancies between an input and its reconstructed version. Their extension to video detection, however, faces two challenges: (i) a fixed reconstructor is not optimized for the detection objective and may yield weak discriminative signals; and (ii) an additional raw-video feature stream is often introduced to model temporal dependencies, increasing architectural overhead. To address these challenges, we develop a detector that learns and exploits reconstruction errors as a unified source of spatiotemporal evidence. First, we jointly optimize a reconstructor and the downstream classifier to produce reconstruction errors that are more informative for detecting AI-generated videos. Second, we show that consecutive reconstruction errors alone carry temporal evidence, and thus a 3D convolution over them captures spatiotemporal evidence without a raw-video stream. Extensive experiments on over 30 existing AI-video generators demonstrate competitive performance against baselines.

Abstract: Video-language models (VLMs) achieve strong performance on video-text retrieval and are increasingly used for egocentric video understanding. Yet, despite gains in zero-shot retrieval, they have shown limitations in compositional understanding, and it remains unclear whether they understand compositional relationships between verbs and nouns. In this work, we introduce Ego-ExBind, a compositional diagnostic protocol for analyzing pretraining exposure and verb-noun binding in egocentric video-text retrieval. Ego-ExBind consists of an Exposure Ledger built from the EgoClip pretraining corpus used by EgoVLPv2, which defines Seen Composition, Unseen Composition, and Unseen Atom splits for EPIC-KITCHENS-100 multi-instance retrieval (MIR). We further probe noun and verb binding channels through controlled noun-swap and verb-swap negatives. We find that zero-shot retrieval is positively associated with pair exposure within seen compositions, even after controlling for the marginal frequencies of verbs and nouns. However, higher pair exposure is associated with larger noun-swap margins and slightly smaller verb-swap margins. This asymmetry motivates us to test whether exposure reweighting can improve retrieval with little change in the measured swap margins. To isolate the effect of exposure input, we keep the trained checkpoint fixed and replace only the exposure input at inference. We demonstrate that this exposure reweighting can improve video-text retrieval without stronger verb-noun compositional understanding, and that the gain depends on the correct sample-level exposure correspondence. By separating exposure-driven retrieval gains from verb-noun binding, Ego-ExBind provides a diagnostic framework for evaluating whether future improvements in egocentric MIR reflect compositional understanding rather than pretraining familiarity.

</details>

Abstract: Single-particle cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM) determines macromolecular structures from noisy particle images. However, rare, transient compositional states and subtle, continuous conformational variation are difficult to resolve. We present CryoGaussian, a Gaussian splatting method for heterogeneous cryo-EM reconstruction that fits an explicit Gaussian density representation directly to the particle images through a differentiable rasterizer rendering orthographic line integrals modulated by the contrast transfer function. The Gaussians are initialized randomly, with their number derived from the estimated spatial support of the molecular density and their minimum size bounded by the Nyquist frequency of the particle images. To capture the compositional and conformational variation across particle images, we learn particle-image and per-Gaussian latents that jointly modulate the base position, amplitude, and scale of each Gaussian. Each structural state is then reconstructed by fitting the Gaussians conditioned on its inferred latent. On CryoBench, CryoGaussian achieves state-of-the-art reconstruction accuracy for both compositional and conformational heterogeneity. On experimental data, it resolves a rare compositional state of the 50S ribosomal subunit (class C4, 1.3% of particles) with rRNA helix 68 docked in the absence of the central protuberance, and continuous conformational motion of the SF3b and helicase subcomplexes of the pre-catalytic spliceosome. We further optimize the rasterizer for additive cryo-EM projection, reducing training time by 12.8-28.0$\times$.

Abstract: World models can imagine how a physical scene evolves over the next few seconds, but growers need to know how a crop will develop over weeks to months: will this rice plant have headed a month from now, and how will it look at harvest? We introduce agricultural world modeling, the task of predicting how the same plant will look at a requested horizon of days to months, from one photo of it, its cultivar, and its camera view. In preliminary analysis, even state-of-the-art image generators get this wrong: they often grow the plant too little or give it the color and form of the wrong growth stage. We argue that they do not work out the plant's developmental state, which the photo reveals only indirectly. We therefore propose AgroWM, which reasons about development before it draws growth. Its reasoning module, PhenoClock, works out the current growth stage, predicts the future one, and writes a Growth Script with numeric targets for height, plant area, and color; its generation module, the Rooted Renderer, then edits the photo to follow this script, adding new organs and colors to the photographed plant. Because pixel scores can prefer an unchanged copy of the photo, we instead check each forecast's traits and developmental timing against the plant's own later photos. On rice, 54.6% of the forecasts of AgroWM are matched to the target stage (Stage R@1), against 34.8% for GPT Image 2.5, 24.0% for Qwen Image 2.1, and 47.4% for the strongest domain-specific baseline, the same backbone fine-tuned without Growth Scripts; 9 to 20 weeks ahead, it still reaches 46.4%, against 35.7% for that baseline. On maize, a crop with a different body plan and stage scale, the same design again scores best on all four measures.

Abstract: Pathology foundation models supply strong representations for whole-slide image classification, yet the confidence attached to their predictions remains hard to trust. Prevailing uncertainty estimators act within a single feature space and thereby overlook the uncertainty that the choice of foundation-model representation itself induces. Abstract: Personalized video generation faces a significant scalability challenge: as the number of custom subjects increases, existing methods suffer from severe identity leakage and semantic entanglement, resulting in videos with blended attributes and incoherent narratives. We identify that this stems from a lack of explicit binding between textual entity phrases and their corresponding visual patterns prior to fusion in diffusion models. To address this, we propose MultiBinder, a novel framework built on a ``bind-then-narrate'' paradigm. Our approach introduces a two-stage conditioning process before video diffusion. Second, a Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) acts as a ``narrative director'' in the Narrative-Aware Subject Arrangement stage, capturing the action of each subject from the text prompt and synthesizes contextualized subject features that encapsulate ``who is doing what'' and preserve role-specific distinctions. These features are then injected into a video diffusion model via dedicated conditioning pathways, ensuring precise attribute allocation. Extensive experiments on both general and newly curated multi-subject benchmarks show that MultiBinder significantly outperforms existing methods in multi-subject identity fidelity.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence requires agents to acquire visual evidence and integrate partial observations into coherent three-dimensional representations. We introduce SpatialScout-V2, which extends SpatialScout's active mapping task from simulation to real residential scenes through three advances: real visual observations, preservation of original scene content, and interactive exploration of recorded views. Given a fixed target inventory, agents autonomously select recorded viewpoints and viewing directions, integrate calibrated RGB observations, and iteratively estimate and revise object centers within a fixed interaction budget. We evaluate the localization accuracy and completeness of object-center maps using mean IoU, IoU-threshold hit rates, and target coverage. Evaluating seven frontier vision-language models (VLMs), we find that six achieve an all-target mean IoU below 20\%. Even the best-performing model, Claude Opus 5.5, reaches only 22.52\%, with just 18.36\% of targets exceeding an IoU of 0.5. Further analysis shows that broad submission coverage can coexist with limited localization accuracy, while target omissions further reduce overall scores. Abstract: Real-time streaming video generation has gained rapid momentum in live content creation and interactive media. Deploying such systems in practice requires flexible scaling factors to accommodate diverse source resolutions, target displays, and computational budgets. However, extending fixed-scale generative video super-resolution (VSR) to varying target scales poses two challenges: downsampling from a larger fixed scale incurs redundant cost, while re-optimizing the diffusion transformer (DiT) is costly and alters the shared generative prior. To address both, we propose FlexVSR, a decoupled framework for continuous-scale streaming generative VSR with supervision at only a few scales. Our key insight is that naively resampling fixed-scale latents onto a new target grid induces scale-dependent input mismatch, which can be effectively addressed through parameter-efficient, scale-aware adaptation of the latent interface without updating the DiT. To this end, FlexVSR introduces ReLSA (Refined Latent Scale Adaptation), a lightweight latent adapter that constructs and refines target-grid latents before they enter the DiT, enabling a shared frozen VSR prior to generalize across continuous scales with only 0.274M trainable parameters and an adaptation cost of 7.3 H100 GPU-hours, while introducing no additional temporal dependencies and preserving the native causal streaming protocol. Extensive experiments demonstrate competitive or superior reconstruction and temporal quality, lower latency than most native-scale streaming baselines and offline methods, and effective generalization to unseen continuous scales, filling the continuous-scale gap in real-time streaming generative VSR.

</details>

Abstract: Images under non-uniform coupled degradations require diverse restoration capabilities for different degraded regions. Existing methods employ globally uniform operations, potentially overlooking regional differences and causing incomplete restoration or even damage to image content. Moreover, a degraded region may require successive restoration actions, and earlier actions may alter residual degradation characteristics or introduce artifacts, potentially hindering subsequent restoration. To address these challenges, we propose RegionAgent, an adaptive restoration framework that trains a Vision-Language Model (VLM) through two-stage preference learning to generate a region-specific restoration action at each iteration. Specifically, we first train the VLM to select an action suited to the current region through step-level Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), using preferences derived from immediate restoration feedback to supervise only the differing component in each action pair. To account for the effects of current actions on subsequent restoration, we then apply trajectory-level DPO, using preferences derived from complete trajectory scores to supervise the sole differing action in each trajectory pair. Furthermore, as conventional image-pair datasets do not directly provide preferences over regional restoration decisions, we construct a regional restoration preference dataset comprising 10K action pairs and 20K trajectory pairs for two-stage VLM training. Experiments across multiple datasets demonstrate strong restoration performance under both mixed and single degradations. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of two-stage preference learning in improving restoration quality.

</details>

Abstract: Semantic segmentation gives an autonomous vehicle a dense, pixel-level description of its surroundings and is a core part of scene understanding. Most segmentation networks consist of an encoder (the backbone) and a decoder, yet the backbone is often chosen without a systematic comparison. This work studies this choice on the BDD100K driving dataset. The effect of the backbone depends strongly on the decoder: the same EfficientNet-B4 gives 42.6% mIoU with PSPNet but 62.6% with FPN, and the gap between the best and the worst backbone ranges from 12.0 points (U-Net) to 42.5 points (FPN). The results and the scope of this study are discussed.

Abstract: Thinking with images (TwI) empowers vision-language models (VLMs) to overcome perceptual bottlenecks by actively cropping and zooming into fine-grained visual details, promising both higher accuracy and interpretable, region-grounded reasoning. However, our analysis shows that the improved accuracy of TwI models does not guarantee faithful tool use; they frequently invoke tools without actually relying on the zoomed region, undermining the reliability of tool-assisted reasoning. We trace this unfaithful behaviour to the training data: when questions are answerable without zooming, the model can answer correctly while ignoring the returned evidence, and this problem is exacerbated during training as the model becomes better at answering without tools. To keep zooming useful throughout training, we adapt the training data to the model's evolving capability and train on questions where correct zoomed evidence can still help the model answer correctly. This selection is supported by a data synthesis pipeline that localises small objects and constructs verifiable questions about their fine details, and complemented by an adaptiveness reward that favours tool-free answers when zooming is unnecessary. The experimental results show that our method substantially improves reasoning faithfulness and achieves state-of-the-art performance on visual search benchmarks (+11.6% on V*, +7.9% on HR-Bench 4K, and +9.4% on HR-Bench 8K). On multimodal hallucination benchmarks, our model also outperforms the compared TwI baselines. These results suggest that improving TwI faithfulness does not sacrifice accuracy and that both can be learnt from the right training data without directly supervising the reasoning process.

</details>

Abstract: Recently, video world models have advanced interactive generation, with large models offering higher quality and smaller models enabling faster inference. Existing speculative methods combine their strengths through fast draft generation and selective target regeneration guided by rewards on decoded frames. However, verification covers only the external scorer's evaluation dimensions and adds decoding and scoring costs. Moreover, these scores provide no correction, requiring costly target regeneration for rejected drafts. To address these limitations, we propose Speculative World Model (SpecWM), a unified, training-free inference framework for streaming world models. It verifies draft chunks using the target's latent velocity field. Verification also yields a reusable correction; two thresholds select Accept, Refine, or Reject. Specifically, one-step refinement reuses it to improve perceptual quality, while three-step refinement strengthens camera following and trajectory consistency. These findings motivate transition-aware refinement. On WBench Navigation, SpecWM improves visual quality at 1.63$\times$ speedup over the target and raises NavScore from the draft's 75.99 to 85.63, achieving the best quality-speed-control trade-off. Abstract: Humanoid agents in everyday environments are expected to react to diverse cues as humans do, with semantically appropriate and physically executable behaviors. Existing cue-driven reaction generation methods support only a narrow range of cue modalities and are limited to purely kinematic generation, leaving the resulting reactions susceptible to physical artifacts. To this end, we present MixReact, the first unified framework for physics-based reactive humanoid control from text, audio, and video cues, integrating a Discrete--Continuous Mixture of Transformers (DisCo-MoT) for state prediction with a History-aware Action Transformer (HAT) for physical execution. DisCo-MoT couples a discrete masked-modeling branch with a continuous autoregressive diffusion branch via one-way mixture attention within a MoT architecture. Guidance from discrete context mitigates collapse into averaged behaviors in one-to-many reaction generation, while direct prediction in continuous state space bypasses the decoding bottleneck of discrete representations. HAT then maps the predicted next state and observed execution history to executable actions, enabling robust closed-loop control in physics-based simulation. Experiments across all three modalities demonstrate consistent improvements over the baselines in fidelity, diversity, and physical consistency, while ablations validate the complementary contributions of the components. Our code will be released to facilitate future research. Project page: https://binlee26.github.io/MixReact_Page/

Abstract: Reliable extraction of calcium-dependent fluorescence traces in calcium imaging sequences requires identifying pixels whose fluorescence intensity reflects the same underlying cellular activity. We present a deterministic temporal embedding framework that maps pixel-wise fluorescence traces to a low-dimensional representation in which temporally coherent sources form spatially coherent regions. Starting from a fuzzy-neighborhood graph and spectral initialization, we optimize a class-balanced cross-entropy objective using exact sparse attraction and a deterministic particle-mesh decomposition of the dense repulsive interaction with sparse edge compensation, avoiding stochastic negative sampling. The resulting latent representation is spatially decoded into reliable source supports for trace extraction without imposing an explicit cellular shape prior. Experiments on synthetic and in vivo two-photon calcium imaging data demonstrate robust recovery of irregular and spatially touching sources together with efficient deterministic embedding optimization.

Abstract: Source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) adapts a pretrained source model to unlabeled target domains without source data access, yet two critical failure modes remain unaddressed. First, attention drift: source-learned attention maps shift toward spurious or background features under distribution shift, destabilizing feature representations. Second, domain-gap-agnostic initialization: applying a single fixed source model uniformly across all target domains ignores domain gap severity, causing premature overfitting to noisy pseudo-labels under large shifts. We propose DGA-CAFC to address both failures. The Counterfactual Attention-based Feature Consistency (CAFC) module enforces feature stability by minimizing the discrepancy between representations computed under learned attention and those under counterfactual-inspired normalized perturbations, thereby promoting domain-robust features independent of spurious attention shifts. The Domain-Gap Aware Source Model Selection (DGASS) module estimates domain gap severity via target prediction entropy and selects the source model variant whose confidence level best matches the estimated gap, reducing negative transfer and stabilizing pseudo-label quality. Crucially, CAFC transfers beyond SFDA: applied as a lightweight regularizer to standard domain generalization objectives without target data. Extensive experiments across SFDA and DG benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently improves over recent baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness and generality of the proposed framework. Project page is available at https://visdomlab.github.io/DGA_CAFC

</details>

Abstract: Short-video platforms rank new uploads before anyone has watched them, using content towers that read a video's cover, title and frames. Existing attacks on content-aware recommenders perturb pixels or features and ignore platform re-encoding, and existing defenses train against norm-bounded perturbations that neither match real edits nor preserve recommendation quality. We show with REAP, a realizable attack through {a simulated platform channel (H.264 transcoding, rescaling, cropping and JPEG)}, that packaging edits raise a cold video by 32.0 rank-percentile points in unrelated audiences. We then observe that cold-start ranking does not need the packaging at all: a tower that sees only the video body ranks cold items best. Building on this, we propose Evidence-First Ranking (EFR), which scores cold videos from their body, admits packaging only through a support-gated residual bounded by $γ$, and merges cold and warm rankings by rank percentile, yielding an exact certificate against any packaging edit. On MicroLens-100K, EFR cuts the attack gain by 81% and raises cold-start Recall@100 from 0.1133 to 0.170, while keeping the merged NDCG@10 within 4% of the undefended system.

Abstract: We present Depth Pro 2, an approach to estimating dense metric depth for images and video at full resolution. By analyzing prior methods for monocular depth estimation, we find that they commonly trade off temporal consistency and boundary sharpness: multi-frame methods favor consistency, single-frame methods favor sharpness. To overcome this trade-off, we train a single, scalable network that processes both images and video and predicts depth maps at native resolution. Abstract: Hierarchical text detection aims to localize words, text lines, and paragraphs while parsing their structural relationships. It provides essential structural cues for visual text understanding. To maximize accuracy, existing methods typically rely on complex multi-scale feature fusion necks and heavy-weight heads, resulting in bloated architectures and low inference efficiency. In this paper, we argue that a sufficiently pre-trained backbone can effectively replace these sophisticated external modules, while simple geometric inclusion suffices for hierarchical text structural parsing. Thus, we propose Prism, a minimalist framework for hierarchical text detection. Prism performs multi-granularity detection by attaching parallel lightweight heads to a single-scale backbone feature map, covering words, text lines, and paragraphs, and reconstructs the structural hierarchy via an efficient geometric inclusion algorithm. In addition, we systematically compare representative lightweight head archetypes and show that pixel-level regression offers the best accuracy-efficiency trade-off in this setting. Experiments on multiple public benchmarks show that our significantly simplified architecture achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance with remarkable efficiency. Specifically, it runs $1.7\times$ faster than LRANet on CTW1500 while achieving higher accuracy, and surpasses the current SOTA method, OmniParser V2, by over 6 PQ points on the hierarchical HierText dataset. Abstract: Videos reveal how the world changes, but rarely the actions that cause those changes. Latent action learning recovers such supervision from visual transitions, yet often learns the underlying dynamics largely from scratch. We ask whether a pretrained video generator can instead provide the prior for both inverse and forward dynamics. We introduce O$^{2}$LaF, built on a simple principle: one prior, two dynamics. We initialize both models from the same pretrained video DiT and adapt them to their distinct roles: a feed-forward transition encoder with learnable action queries for inverse dynamics, and a causal, action-conditioned video generator for forward dynamics. By controlling information access and optimizing both dynamics end-to-end through a compact bottleneck, O$^{2}$LaF learns transferable actions from unlabeled videos. Across navigation and robotic manipulation, O$^{2}$LaF sets a new state of the art in few-shot action probing and enables stronger zero-shot action transfer across visual contexts and embodiments. Beyond generative video priors, we further extend our framework to predictive representation priors such as V-JEPA2, achieving competitive performance. Our findings suggest a broader paradigm for latent-action learning: repurposing pretrained video models to discover transferable actions and predict their consequences.

Abstract: Analogue gauges remain prevalent in manufacturing industries for measuring physical quantities, yet manual monitoring by operators introduces human error and operational inefficiency. This research developed and evaluated an automated gauge reading system using computer vision and deep learning techniques. Three hybrid approaches were implemented and compared: OpenCV with Scikit-Image (traditional computer vision), OpenCV with ResNet18, and OpenCV with VGG19 (deep learning approaches). Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training substantially improves the visual quality, text rendering, and compositional accuracy of diffusion transformers. Because capabilities such as rendering requested text or composing multiple objects are inherently prompt-dependent, a natural hypothesis is that improving them requires learning complex, input-dependent transformations. To isolate how much of these gains an input-independent shift explains, we probe each RL-tuned model with a deliberately simple parameterization: a fixed set of steering vectors added to the block outputs of the frozen base model. On SD3.5-M, these vectors hold 65x fewer parameters than the RL-tuned LoRA (0.29M against 18.8M). We fit them to match the velocity predictions of public RL-tuned checkpoints, with no reward in the objective. Although the vectors are identical for every prompt, token, and sampling seed, they recover 90.0-96.3% of the reward gains of six RL-tuned image models, and 89.8% and 111.9% on two RL-tuned video models. Initialized at zero and trained with online RL, the vectors alone exceed the reward gains of weight-based RL on text rendering (OCR) and human preference (PickScore) on SD3.5-M. A constant shift thus suffices for most of the gain of policy-gradient RL post-training: without new input-dependent transformations, it steers the pretrained generator toward what it can already compute.

Abstract: Tracking objects through transformations is essential for understanding real-world dynamics. Recent work has extended traditional object tracking to detect state changes. However, existing methods require an initial object prompt and detect only its decompositions, missing assembly events, which begin with multiple objects rather than one. We therefore introduce a prompt-free formulation of this task, where the goal is to recover all object tracks and detect both decomposition and assembly events given only a video. Our key insight is that assembly is decomposition in reverse. Building on this insight, we present Atlas, a zero-shot system that constructs and jointly reasons over forward and reverse spatiotemporal partitions to recover object tracks and their transformations. We further introduce OmniST, a benchmark with dense annotations of objects and both transformation types. On OmniST, Atlas recovers more than twice as many state changes as prior methods while remaining competitive on existing benchmarks that only evaluate decomposition.
