Abstract: Coding agents built on large language models (LLMs) now help develop many models published as general solutions. Should generality instead reside in the agent that builds a model for each new task? We examine this question in prognostics and health management (PHM), where every model must work on unseen equipment. Our deployment-style benchmark, NextUnit-Bench, covers 24 public datasets split into 58 folds that hold out units or operating conditions; each agent has two hours to deliver an executable predictor, then run on the hidden units. First, frontier agents outperform AutoML, which reaches less than half the best agent's score, and faithful implementations of published methods on four datasets, of which only 6 of 30 beat the strongest agent's own design; published accuracies, where reproducible, rest on the papers' own conventions, and a single convention, capping the remaining life of test engines, can halve the reported error. Second, the agents' advantage is a design made on each machine's data; once made, a plain-text plan of a few hundred words carries most of the gap between two frontier agents, and given a weaker agent's plan, the strongest agent falls to that agent's level, often to identical predictions. Before touching the data, however, it matters little whose plan an agent carries out, and domain toolkits appear to help neither: what sets the strongest apart is how much it learns from the data. Third, such designs reach only implementers that can execute them: given the strongest agent's designs, a locally deployable open-weight model delivers half as often, even when they are lightened, and scores no higher than without them. Generality, we argue, is not disappearing but moving, from the model to the modeler.

</details>

Abstract: Adversarial example detectors often fail when attackers explicitly attempt to bypass them. We propose a dual-model approach that keeps the original classifier fixed and trains a separate verifier to assess its predictions. During training, we identify patterns of internal neural network activations that distinguish genuine examples of each class from adversarial examples targeting that class. These patterns, called activation signatures, guide the generation of challenging adversarial examples that mislead the classifier, challenge the verifier, and internally resemble the target class. The verifier learns to recognize these examples while preserving its performance on clean inputs through knowledge distillation. At inference, the verifier evaluates the classifier's prediction without requiring activation signatures. We evaluate the approach on publicly available datasets against several adaptive attacks, including attacks unseen during training. The proposed verifier substantially outperforms directly attacked confidence-based and feature-based detectors under the evaluated L-infinity attacks. Ablation studies show that adaptive verifier training is the main source of this improvement, while signature guidance primarily benefits clean accuracy. Additional L2 evaluations reveal limitations in robustness across perturbation types.

</details>

Abstract: Graph coloring, which entails assigning colors to nodes such that no adjacent nodes have the same color, is a fundamental combinatorial optimization problem. In general, however, finding a node coloring with no monochromatic edges given a fixed palette is NP-hard. This motivates learning-based methods that can learn to exploit recurring structure across instances to efficiently produce high-quality colorings. Crucially, the validity of a coloring is invariant to permutations of the colors, and we show that exploiting this symmetry reduces the size of the effective state space over which the model has to learn its policy. At the same time, the coloring algorithm must be able to break some symmetries, for example to assign different colors to two adjacent nodes belonging to the same automorphism orbit. In this paper, we introduce Scone, a model that uses a color-equivariant graph neural network to construct colorings sequentially, allowing information from previous assignments to propagate and break node symmetries. Scone achieves perfect solution quality with low inference times across several graph coloring tasks, including on sudoku_extreme and register allocations, and showcases generalization to graph families not encountered during training.

</details>

Abstract: Modern recommendation systems face substantial memory pressure, largely from the sparse embedding layer that maps high-cardinality categorical features into dense vectors. In-training embedding pruning (ITEP) removes less important embedding rows during training, yielding end-to-end memory savings but sometimes degrading accuracy because per-table pruning ratios are set by size-based heuristics. We use feature importance (FI) to guide these ratios (FI-ITEP), assigning higher pruning to tables tied to less important features. Across three large-scale recommendation models, FI-ITEP achieves 20--40% total memory reduction while matching or improving accuracy relative to percentile-based ITEP. Because the feature-importance signal requires minimal resources to compute, these memory savings translate into concrete systems benefits: the same models train on fewer GPU trainers (e.g., 112 to 80) with higher per-GPU throughput, saving 1,000+ GPU-hours per model launch. Abstract: AI systems operating near the limits of their capabilities can maximize utility while minimizing the risk of error by abstaining or deferring questions they are not confident about to a human or a stronger model. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have been post-trained to estimate confidence in their own answers. To achieve this, methods reinforce confidence scores based on the correctness signal to each question-answer pair individually, with rewards that encourage assigning confidence 1 to correct answers and 0 to incorrect answers. However, estimating confidence scores is useful in that it enables selecting only the most confident answers, to maximize the share of solved questions while accommodating the user's risk tolerance. Assigning confidence 1 to correct answers and 0 to incorrect ones is theoretically optimal, but unattainable in practice and detrimental for selective prediction with a non-zero risk tolerance: confidences pushed to 0 and 1 carry no information about how answers compare to one another. We therefore introduce CARE (Cross-question Abstention Reward Estimation), which computes rewards over a set of question-answer pairs: At each of several risk tolerances, the confidences across the set induce an abstention threshold, and each confidence is rewarded for lying on the correct side of it. Since the threshold itself comes from the set, every verbalized confidence is reinforced relative to other questions' confidence, not against its own binary correctness label. On math, CARE increases the coverage at 5% risk (C@5) across MATH-500, GSM8K, and Big-Math-Digits from 0.8% to 42.4%, compared to existing pointwise correctness-matching rewards. When trained on multi-hop Wikipedia QA from HotpotQA, CARE improves C@5 in out-of-distribution general-knowledge, reasoning, and math benchmarks (including CommonsenseQA, GPQA, SimpleQA, and TriviaQA) from 12.3% to 23.6%.

</details>

Abstract: Split federated learning (SFL) distributes neural networks across clients and a server, coupling class-specific knowledge across their representations. This coupling complicates split federated unlearning (SFU), where class-wise forgetting must remove target knowledge while preserving retained utility under heterogeneous representation spaces and asymmetric update constraints. We propose SplitReAlign, a partition-aware representation realignment framework for class-wise SFU. It constructs endpoint-specific discriminative subspaces coordinated through a shared semantic anchor, selectively realigning forgotten representations while penalizing changes outside the active subspaces. To accommodate asymmetric update constraints, SplitReAlign leverages gradients through frozen servers for Client-only updates and fixed client-feature statistics for Server-only guidance, while Joint updates optimize both partitions. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 under heterogeneous client distributions demonstrate that SplitReAlign achieves the lowest average gap to retraining among all compared methods across six settings. Notably, Server-only achieves the closest agreement with retraining on both datasets, highlighting the effectiveness of partition-aware guidance under restricted updates.

Abstract: A Bayesian learner may retain an issued action while updating its posterior, trading predictive loss against adjustment charges. We assume population curvature, boundary control, an order-$1/n$ lower bound on expected posterior uncertainty about the optimal action, and an observable unbiased gradient witness whose squared norms are uniformly integrable. Sample losses may be unbounded or nonconvex. Cumulative expected excess measures the loss increase relative to current predictive Bayes actions. Here $J$ indexes logarithmic time. The criterion is finiteness of a prefix-constrained allocation of tracking-error allowances across action-distance scales, equivalently its decreasing-weight dual. Adaptive timing and private randomization do not enlarge the attainable region. For each feasible pair, a horizon-independent deterministic checkpoint policy attains the profile with lifetime-cost moments of every positive order. For excess $O((\log T)^r)$, $0<r<1$, every policy needs $Ω((\log T)^{2-r})$ revisions in expectation, and a charge-independent schedule achieves a matching pathwise upper bound for every feasible charge. We verify the assumptions for logistic decision models with nondegenerate covariates, finite second moments and a locally positive smooth prior density, including fixed-ridge models with unbounded parameter priors.

Abstract: Residual reinforcement learning corrects an imitation-trained base policy using a limited budget of environment interaction. In a task with several phases, this budget must be divided among the phases, and the phase in which the base policy fails is not necessarily the phase in which a correction most improves task completion. We formalize this distinction through the completion gain: the change in full-task completion probability when, during a single phase, a state-feedback controller replaces selected components of the base policy's action. We decompose the completion gain into the probability of reaching the phase and the conditional improvement in completion from the start of the phase, and we estimate it from paired episodes. The positive estimates determine which action components the residual policy corrects and how training episodes are distributed across phases. In stochastic point-mass docking experiments, most failures occurred in the final docking phase, whereas the largest completion gain occurred in the preceding channel phase; gain-based allocation improved completion over allocation proportional to failure counts and, in a variant with a longer approach, over uniform allocation at half of the budget. In simulated bimanual robotic assembly, the completion-gain estimates were positive only for insertion and release, although few failures occurred during release; in a second assembly configuration, a residual policy trained in the release phase improved completion from held-out states at the start of release by 16.9%.

</details>

Abstract: In regulated domains such as lending, the two most salient fairness concepts are disparate impact and disparate treatment: disparate impact is generally defined by differential rates of adverse outcomes between demographic groups, while disparate treatment is generally defined by the use of demographic information or proxies to issue decisions. There already exist established methods to quantitatively detect and mitigate disparate impact; however, there has been less research on doing the same for disparate treatment. From a practitioner's perspective, the manner in which a conditional independence test is formulated and operationalized to identify proxies is nuanced and underexplored. As Machine Learning (ML) systems increase in scope and application in regulated domains, practitioners need a defensible framework to identify proxies at scale. Current disparate treatment frameworks typically rely upon some combination of quantitative and qualitative review of models' inputs to identify proxies. Features that are identified as proxies are then typically removed from the model in practice, and predictive performance degrades in favor of fairness through unawareness. Here, we propose a two-part framework that reduces the disparate treatment risk of a feature and keeps the predictive performance that removal of the feature loses. First, we propose a quantitative test for proxy capacity that identifies whether some of a feature's values reveal membership in a particular demographic group. Second, we propose a pre-processing methodology that adjusts those proxy features to reduce their proxy capacity. We show empirically on synthetic and public datasets that this framework identifies features that proxy for demographic information and reduces their proxy capacity while the adjusted feature retains task-relevant signal (i.e. improves predictive performance versus removal).

</details>

Abstract: This paper proposes a lightweight policy model to mitigate the difficulty of handling higher-dimensional action spaces in reinforcement learning (RL), alongside an algorithm to effectively train it. To address this, we focus on drifting models, which can implicitly represent complex distributions and infer actions in a single forward pass. However, while drifting models can be straightforwardly trained via backpropagation of the Q-gradient, this alone cannot be expected to appropriately capture the desired action coordination. Therefore, this paper constructs a three-step learning algorithm to encourage the action coordination. First, the source distribution of the drifting model is co-optimized as a diagonal policy model. This conditions the drifting model to attach the coordination, which cannot be expressed by a diagonal distribution, as residuals. Second, a discriminator model before and after drifting is introduced with adversarial training for a regularization term. This actively promotes the extraction of the coordination that fundamentally separates the two distributions. Third, best-of-N sampling is applied for action selection during rollouts in order to give directionality toward/beyond the regions where the coordination is insufficiently extracted/sufficiently discovered. The proposed method consistently improves the sample efficiency and the final control performance over the conventional diagonal distribution modeling across three locomotion tasks with high-dimensional action spaces. Furthermore, the analysis indicates that this improvement is achieved by reducing variance in certain action dimensions, effectively coupling them with other actions as action coordination.

</details>

Abstract: Humans act in the visual world by inferring the 3D layout behind a 2D view, predicting how an action changes it, and correcting themselves after seeing the result. Can multimodal large language models (MLLMs) do the same? CAPTCHAs offer a natural test, since humans solve them through this very loop, clicking and dragging on the screen and adjusting after each result. Yet existing CAPTCHA benchmarks are largely solved and rarely spatial, while spatial benchmarks rarely require action. We therefore present TuringSpace, a synthetic web environment of 125 interactive CAPTCHAs that span five spatial abilities and must be solved through clicks and drags on the screen. Every task is a program that generates new instances with a checker and an oracle, so the tasks also form a scalable training gym. Humans solve 94.1% of the tasks, whereas the best model, GPT-6 Astra, still fails 25.9% of them, and the best open-weight model, Kimi K3, fails 68.0%. Our further analysis shows that most failures come from misreading the screenshot, and that model reasoning rarely corrects a wrong belief but hallucinates evidence to defend it. To test whether such spatial abilities can be learned, we fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B on this gym. Its success rate rises from 1.6% to 33.1%, above Kimi K3, and it improves on all seven out-of-distribution benchmarks, including a 9.9-point gain on the real-world indoor videos of VSI-Bench. These results show that grounding spatial intelligence in screen actions exposes what MLLMs lack and offers a scalable way to teach it, with gains that transfer to real-world scenes. We will release TuringSpace to support further study.

Abstract: Protein language models (PLMs) are commonly pretrained with likelihood-based objectives such as masked language modeling or denoising. These objectives capture sequence-level regularities, but can produce representations in which functional signals are entangled with non-functional variation (e.g., sequence identity, compositional bias, and frequent local motifs), creating a readout bottleneck for downstream function prediction. We introduce Functional Semantic Alignment via Representation Rectification (SAVR), a contrastive post-training method using Pfam family co-membership as a homology-guided discrete semantic signal to reshape PLM representations. SAVR reduces directional dominance and redistributes variance across embedding dimensions, decreasing variance concentration, and increasing effective dimensionality of the embedding covariance spectrum. Without observing any task-specific labels during post-training, SAVR achieves state-of-the-art results on Enzyme Commission (EC) benchmarks and competitive results on Gene Ontology (GO) benchmarks under frozen-backbone evaluation. These results suggest that homology-guided contrastive post-training can rectify PLM representation geometry and improve sequence-based protein function prediction.

Abstract: Fine-grained visual understanding requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to recognize small visual entities and understand their relationships. Scene graphs offer a natural way to represent this information, organizing visual entities as nodes and their relationships as edges. However, we identify two challenges: existing scene graph benchmarks provide insufficient coverage of fine-grained entities and the relationships among them, and existing methods for fine-grained visual understanding have yet to effectively leverage such graph-structured information in MLLMs. To address these challenges, we introduce Fine-Grained Multimodal Scene Graph Benchmark (FG-GraphBench) and FiGRA. FG-GraphBench is a benchmark comprising three complementary tasks: fine-grained visual question answering (FG-QA), image captioning (FG-Caption), and image description generation (FG-Description). We develop a two-stage construction pipeline that discovers and grounds fine-grained entities and establishes their relations to construct the fine-grained multimodal scene graphs (MSGs) in FG-GraphBench. Building on these MSGs, we propose FiGRA, a Fine-grained Graph Representation Augmentation method for MLLMs. FiGRA adopts a two-stage training strategy. It encodes multimodal entity and relation features as continuous graph tokens, and integrates them into the MLLM. Experiments show that FiGRA significantly outperforms strong baselines across three tasks of FG-GraphBench. Abstract: Genomic foundation models provide variant-level evidence, but connecting that evidence to phenotype hypotheses requires accounting for uncertainty throughout structured reasoning. An interpretable learned energy scores admissible gene--disease--phenotype routes, query uncertainty controls the target temperature, and path probabilities are aggregated into phenotype hypotheses. This formulation makes evidence quality an explicit determinant of hypothesis breadth while retaining traceable multi-path support. Abstract: We study the problem of computational efficiency for linear bandits in high-dimensional settings with a finite arm set. In linear bandits, the increase in the dimension $d$ of the feature vectors leads to growing computational costs of $O(d^2)$ at each round of update. Traditional low-rank approximation methods such as SOFUL reduce computation via fixed-size matrix sketching, yet run the risk of incurring vacuous linear regret when the spectral tail of the data is heavy and the approximation rank is inadequately selected. To guarantee regret convergence and effectively reduce computational costs, we introduce a clustering mechanism and propose the Coordinate Basis Linear Bandit (CBL) algorithm. Our method preserves the full covariance information in each cluster by maintaining an exact orthonormal basis of the cluster's column space together with a low-dimensional coordinate representation, which guarantees robust sublinear regret without spectral-tail vulnerabilities. It performs cluster switching by assigning a sentinel for each cluster, and reduces per-round update computation to $O(\bar r^2)$ in the cluster's effective dimension $\bar r=\min(r,l)$, where $r$ is the intrinsic rank of the data and $l<d$ is a tunable rank bound.

</details>

Abstract: Combining fine-tuned models by composing their parameter updates has become a standard alternative to multi-task training. We formalize composability as a property of a collection of task vectors and prove that the excess loss of the merged model is controlled by an interference functional of the cross-task curvature overlap and the signed alignment of the updates. In the quadratic regime this functional equals the excess loss, giving a criterion that is necessary as well as sufficient and that measures the angle between task vectors in the curvature metric rather than the Euclidean one. Fine-tuning under a curvature-guided regularizer -- constructed from curvature statistics of the kind memory-efficient optimizers maintain -- keeps displacement in the base curvature metric below a ceiling set by the penalty coefficient at every iterate, at the standard $O(1/\sqrt{T})$ non-convex rate. Under a single Loewner comparison between the base statistic and the task curvatures, this control converts into a ceiling on the interference functional, inversely proportional to the penalty coefficient, so that the curvature term of the bound is controlled during training. A first-order term, present because regularized solutions are not stationary for the task loss, is not reduced in the same way. Experiments on an exact non-quadratic model confirm the budget and the ceiling, show the first-order term dominating for strong penalties, and find the curvature-guided penalty slightly better than an isotropic one. In a three-seed transformer study the functional is rank-correlated with merging degradation, but no more strongly than the Euclidean displacement norm.

Abstract: Inverse electrocardiography (ECG) reconstructs heart-surface potentials from body-surface measurements, enabling noninvasive mapping of cardiac electrical activity. However, the reconstruction requires solving dense and ill-conditioned linear systems, whose computational cost grows rapidly with the refined heart mesh. Quantum computing offers new opportunities for solving such systems. This paper presents a hybrid quantum-classical framework for inverse ECG reconstruction, which, to the best of our knowledge, is the first to apply a quantum linear solver to this problem. We first propose a Laplacian-regularized pre-encoding procedure to reduce the dimension of the system while improving its conditioning. Second, the resulting reduced system is encoded and solved using a coherent variational quantum linear solver (VQLS). Finally, we provide a rigorous theoretical proof establishing that the proposed framework preserves the exact solution of the original reconstruction problem throughout all stages of the computational process. Experimental results on a realistic heart-torso geometry demonstrate that our approach holds significant ability for accurate inverse ECG reconstruction. These findings highlight the potential of quantum solvers for noninvasive cardiac imaging, providing a foundation for further research into quantum-enhanced cardiac assessment and other healthcare applications.

Abstract: Latent crystal generative models typically combine a variational autoencoder (VAE) for learning atom-wise representations with a flow model that transports a simple source distribution to the learned latent distribution. However, previous studies have mainly focused on the quality of generated crystals, leaving the structure and generative utility of the atom-wise latent space largely unexplored. In this work, we show that even a vanilla crystal VAE implicitly organizes atom-wise latent representations according to elemental families, with different families occupying largely separated regions of the latent space. We term this phenomenon emergent chemical semantics. This structure suggests that sampling from a family-specific latent region can bias the decoder toward atoms from the corresponding elemental family. Building on this insight, we propose FamilySpace, a framework for controlling the elemental-family composition of generated crystals. FamilySpace assigns each elemental family a distinct source component, forming a family-indexed multi-source distribution, and trains a flow model using source-target pairs matched by family. In inference, a user-specified family template selects the source component for each atom-wise representation, enabling direct control over the family composition of generated crystals. Experiments on MP-20 and MPTS-52 show that FamilySpace achieves competitive crystal-generation performance while matching prescribed elemental-family compositions. Moreover, selected family templates increase the fraction of generated crystals predicted to be stable or metastable, demonstrating that latent-space composition control can support targeted exploration of promising chemical regions without additional optimization techniques such as reinforcement learning. Code is available at https://github.com/hhw11/FamilySpace

Abstract: Neural networks encode learned capabilities in their weights, yet extending those capabilities with explicit task knowledge remains a fundamental challenge. Here we show that Weight-Space Programming (WSP), in which executable programs read, construct or modify a network's weights directly, can install new capabilities within trained neural networks. WeightCode, a WSP framework, uses a frozen language model to write executable weight programs that install new computations into trained neural networks using task knowledge and their existing weights, without gradient-based training. Once installed, the new capabilities run independently of the language model. Given a grammar or eight demonstrations, weight programs enable previously unseen command compositions (0% to 99.8% exact match) and a new pickup action (0% to 98.4% success), while preserving the original command and navigation capabilities. In a Vision Transformer, causal interventions show that installed noise-correction gates drive robustness gains, while their selective activation preserves clean accuracy. Rerun on five independently trained Vision Transformers without further language model calls, ten weight programs retain 83-110% of their source-network gains on average, whereas copying their fixed weight edits yields negligible gains. Six programs written for an already edited network add 5.23-14.50 points of contrast robustness while retaining the previously installed noise gain. Together, these results establish WSP as a new route to acquiring and accumulating capabilities in trained neural networks through reusable weight programs.

Abstract: Smoothed online learning has recently been studied as a way to bypass hardness results for the fully adversarial setting, which can be overly pessimistic. In this framework, the adversary is constrained to generate contexts from distributions having a bounded density with respect to some fixed base measure $μ$. Prior work makes the strong assumption that $μ$ is known to the learner, with notable exceptions including Block et al. (2024) and Blanchard (2025). In this paper, we study sequential probability assignment (a.k.a., online learning with log loss) with smooth, well-specified data in the more general setting where $μ$ is \emph{unknown}, without the Lipschitz condition imposed in the work of Block et al. (2024) and Blanchard (2025). We prove regret upper bounds against both adaptive and oblivious smoothed adversaries: the adaptive bound is controlled by empirical $\ell_\infty$ Hellinger entropy, while the oblivious bound improves this dependence to empirical $\ell_2$ Hellinger entropy, the notion that has been shown to characterize the complexity of learning with i.i.d. data (Bilodeau et al., 2023). Both bounds are obtained through algorithms based on truncated empirical Hellinger covers. We complement these results with a general lower bound, showing that our upper bounds are essentially tight for some natural classes, which implies a separation in the difficulty of smoothed online learning between regimes where $μ$ is known and where it is unknown.

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently established a new paradigm in machine learning, demonstrating exceptional zero-shot generalization and complex reasoning capabilities. Concurrently, neural network parameter generation has emerged as a promising avenue for rapid task adaptation and model synthesis. Existing weight generation techniques, however, struggle to jointly learn the distribution of diverse models, to encode heterogeneous architectures within a single generator, and to generalize out of distribution. We therefore ask whether pretrained parameters can be encoded into a discrete domain so that the zero-shot generalization LLMs exhibit in text generation can be transferred to weight generation. Following this view, we propose an LLM-inspired approach, Instruction-Guided Parameter Generation (IGPG), that formulates network parameter generation as an autoregressive modeling problem. We first quantize pretrained weights with a VQ-VAE, then train a prefix-conditioned autoregressive generator over the resulting tokens. At inference, the generated weight tokens are mapped back to parameter space by the VQ-VAE decoder. We evaluate the framework across in-distribution generation, cross-dataset transfer, and cross-architecture settings. The proposed method achieves competitive performance in in-distribution generation and provides useful parameter initializations for held-out datasets and architectural configurations, improving downstream performance over random initialization after adaptation. These results suggest that discrete autoregressive modeling provides a promising framework for learning and transferring structure across collections of pretrained neural network parameters.

Abstract: A prompt for in-context learning (ICL) can describe a task in addition to, or instead of, demonstrating it through examples. Yet how task descriptions work remains poorly understood theoretically. Through the Bayesian view of ICL, we model descriptions as informative priors characterized by their specificity and reliability. We adapt the effective sample size (ESS) from Bayesian statistics, quantifying a description's predictive value as an equivalent number of demonstrations. For in-context linear regression, we show that a reliable description's ESS grows with its specificity and with the task's dimension. A description that may be irrelevant, however, gains little from its specificity unless demonstrations are given. In practice, do in-context learners use descriptions as a Bayes-optimal predictor would? Our experiments show that meta-trained Transformers come close to the Bayes-optimal ESS when tested on their training distribution. Pretrained language models, however, make too little use of highly specific descriptions when answering without reasoning; most of them closely match the Bayes-optimal predictor only when reasoning is enabled. These results extend the Bayesian analysis of ICL from demonstrations to descriptions.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate mobile traffic forecasting is crucial for network service management, supporting proactive capacity provisioning, congestion mitigation, and energy-efficient operation. However, many cities and regions are data-scarce (e.g., newly deployed or poorly instrumented areas), thus making traffic prediction a few-shot learning problem and limiting the practicality of standard data-driven models. Existing cross-city solutions primarily rely on data-space alignment to match traffic distributions between data-rich and data-scarce cities, but such alignment is often brittle under real-world distribution shifts, measurement noise, and city-specific bias. To address these limitations, we propose a Conditional Diffusion-based Parameter Generation (CDPG) framework. Instead of aligning raw data, CDPG learns the conditional distribution of high-performing predictor parameters for source cities and directly generates node-specific model parameters for the target city. To support reliable adaptation for network traffic prediction, CDPG conditions parameter generation on multi-modal spatio-temporal context. Specifically, CDPG captures spatial environment cues by combining satellite imagery with large language model (LLM)-derived semantic descriptions, and summarizes complex temporal behaviors via unsupervised pattern clustering. These contextual components guide CDPG in producing parameters tailored to each base station's operational context, thereby improving robustness under cross-city and cross-region shifts. Extensive experiments on three large-scale datasets show that CDPG consistently outperforms state-of-the-art cross-city few-shot baselines by up to 9.72% on standard forecasting metrics and remains robust for few-shot multi-step prediction. The code is available at https://github.com/LingoodBoy/CDPG.

Abstract: Comparative feedback, in which a judge says which of two candidate outcomes is closer to what happened, is a practical way to train language world models that assign probabilities to an action's outcomes. We show that, fitted with a Bradley-Terry objective, it need not teach the true outcome probabilities and can distort accurate ones. On a simulated robot push with enumerated outcome distributions, this objective takes a model that is accurate on unseen layouts (hazard error 2.4 percentage points, pp) and moves 33 to 43 pp of its probability onto outcomes that none of a condition's enumerated branches produces. The cause is the objective, not the comparisons: its optimum, computed before training, predicts where the model ends up, and a likelihood that knows the judge's rule learns from the same comparisons without the shift. The shift is robust: it recurs on unseen conditions, under five candidate laws and a three-fold denser enumeration, and about 29 pp remain without the physically impossible events. Its cost is conditional: with those events it inflates hazard probabilities, and a threshold inherited from the pre-feedback model costs about 3 pp of utility per decision on trained and 1 to 2 pp on unseen conditions, through false rejects; without them the utility loss averages below 1 pp over six seeds, though a small regret in action choice remains. Audits of readout, response and decision trace the effect from probabilities to actions.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise as predictors across various tasks, benefiting from their ability to use information from unstructured context. However, when predictions serve as parameters of downstream optimization problems, higher prediction accuracy does not necessarily translate into lower decision costs. We propose decision-focused reinforcement learning (DFRL), a framework for post-training LLMs to predict optimization parameters using negative downstream decision regret as reward. DFRL trains LLM predictors to minimize the costs of the decisions induced by their predictions, without requiring downstream convexity or differentiability, and integrates readily into existing RL post-training frameworks. To address expensive reward evaluation, we develop two approaches. Heuristic warm-starting uses inexpensive approximate rewards during an initial phase before switching to refinement with exact rewards. Reward correction combines inexpensive estimates with randomized exact evaluations, preserving the expected policy gradient while reducing the expected number of exact solves per update. Experiments on three synthetic optimization problems demonstrate improvements over prediction-loss baselines, while a larger knapsack experiment shows faster early improvement under short training budgets. On inventory planning with H&M transactions and product descriptions, our best configuration, in which the LLM reasons before predicting, reduces regret by 31.4% relative to the SFT baseline and 12.4% relative to the strongest evaluated conventional forecasting baseline.

Abstract: Multiscale convection-diffusion-reaction (CDR) systems couple spatial transport with local stiff reaction kinetics, making their numerical simulation computationally demanding. Conventional neural surrogates require costly coupled CDR training data, and their parameter counts scale with the number of transported variables, limiting scalability to high-dimensional CDR systems. Here we propose a PDE-Structured Transport Operator (PSTO) that learns convection--diffusion dynamics solely from reaction-free data. By embedding the PDE structure into the neural architecture, PSTO generalizes to full CDR systems at inference without retraining. A single transport operator block is shared across all transported variables and refined through relaxed fixed-point iterations, performing an implicit-like correction in latent space. This improves stability during recursive multi-step prediction without adding trainable parameters. We evaluate PSTO on three benchmarks: a canonical 2D CDR system, a multiscale Robertson counterflow flame, and a multiscale hydrogen planar jet flame. In every case, PSTO reduces the mean relative $L_2$ error after 100 coupled steps by at least 50% compared to the strongest neural baseline. Ablation studies on the fixed-point iterations and detailed error decomposition further confirm that PSTO achieves both high one-step accuracy and long-term stability in coupled rollouts. Time-step sensitivity analysis shows that PSTO maintains accuracy even with a tenfold larger time step, whereas baseline accuracies decay rapidly as the time step increases. This framework enables efficient, modular modeling of multiscale CDR dynamics by separating transport learning from the treatment of local kinetics.

</details>

Abstract: The concentration and training-time constancy of the neural tangent kernel (NTK) depend on the interplay between network width, depth, and initialization. While joint depth-width scaling has been studied in fully-connected, residual, and recurrent networks, its effects in convolutional neural networks (CNNs) remain less understood. Abstract: We formulate undersampling mask design for Cartesian magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) as a feature subset selection problem. Each sampling line is represented by a binary feature indicating whether it is included in each candidate mask. Using candidate masks and their training-set evaluations, we estimate mutual information (MI) and conditional mutual information (CMI). These estimates and a sampling-count penalty define a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) model. The model is solved using simulated annealing and quantum annealing to determine a feature subset and its corresponding sampling mask. We assess the selected subsets on the test set through zero-filled reconstruction in Fourier undersampling simulations using fastMRI knee magnitude reference images, with patient-disjoint training and test sets. At all six tested target sampling rates from 15% to 40%, the resulting reconstructions have lower mean root mean square error than those obtained with four conventional sampling methods. They also have higher mean structural similarity than those obtained using univariate mutual-information ranking at all tested rates. These results support information-theoretic feature selection as an approach to MRI undersampling mask design under the tested zero-filled simulation settings.

Abstract: The products of chemical reactions are usually complex mixtures that must be separated into their components before interpretable $^1$H-NMR spectra can be obtained. Direct analysis of the spectra of crude mixtures would accelerate reaction analysis and facilitate the exploration of new chemical space. We present NIMIS, a structure-agnostic, attention-based deep learning framework that groups the peaks of a 1D $^1$H-NMR mixture spectrum according to the component from which they originate. On held-out molecules it reaches a peak-to-component assignment accuracy of 82.28% for noise-free input and 77.34% with 10% integral noise, with a mean NIMIS score of 0.83 for correct assignments. NIMIS outperforms a conventional Transformer and simple baselines, and its performance carries over to a scaffold-disjoint evaluation. Abstract: Domain generalization (DG) aims to train models on multiple source domains with distinct distributions to ensure robust performance on unseen target domains. In doing so, it elevates the concept of generalization from the traditional sample level to the distribution level. Existing theoretical frameworks introduce a domain space endowed with a meta-distribution to extend classical generalization analysis to this new setting. However, these approaches typically treat the domain space as devoid of geometry. In reality, the domain space is an inherently infinite-dimensional, nonlinear abstract space composed of probability distributions, and its complex geometric properties fundamentally determine generalization behavior. Consequently, existing geometry-free theories of domain generalization have a fundamental limitation. They lack a formal characterization of what distinguishes distribution-level generalization from sample-level generalization, leaving the inherent difficulty of generalizing across domains largely unexplained. To bridge this gap, we formalize the domain space as the Wasserstein space over the representation space. Building on the geometric properties of this space (specifically the $\mathrm{CD}(K,\infty)$ curvature theory) we derive a novel, geometry-dependent DG error bound. By introducing an additional curvature-dependent confidence term, this bound formally disentangles the two levels of generalization effects and reveals the essential nature that domain generalization is affected by both the invariance and the curvature of the representation space. Finally, we experimentally validate the effect of curvature on domain generalization.

Abstract: Deep learning models that combine genotypes with neuroimaging predict neurological diseases more accurately than either modality alone. Their clinical use, however, requires identifying which genetic variants a prediction is based on. Path-based attribution methods like Integrated Gradients address this by integrating the model's gradient from a baseline to the input. These methods assume the model accepts any point along that path as a valid input. Genotype models violate this assumption. They map the three discrete allele dosages \{0,1,2\} to vectors through a learned embedding defined only at those values, so a straight line passes through fractional genotypes at which no embedding, and therefore no gradient, exists. Attribution must therefore proceed in embedding space where the choice of path can affect the resulting attribution. We show that the commonly used per-variant estimator does not generally satisfy completeness. Additive separability guarantees completeness for this estimator, but interactions between variants generally break that property. Instead, we integrate along piecewise-linear embedding-space paths connecting genotype waypoints observed in the training cohort. Each path satisfies completeness in the continuum although the interiors of its segments are not necessarily observed. Our method improves faithfulness over Integrated Gradients and discretized Integrated Gradients under both perturbation protocols, achieves the strongest results for Parkinson's disease classification under support-preserving perturbations, and is competitive with a support-background Shapley baseline under per-variant perturbations. It also shows higher seed stability and better recovery of planted non-additive effects in a synthetic benchmark.

</details>

Abstract: Model explanations are increasingly used as scientific evidence, yet it remains unclear what they license scientists to conclude beyond the data on which they were constructed. When a fine-grained explanation fails to replicate, the failure may be attributed to noisy measurement of its target, raising a fundamental question: does reliable measurement imply a reproducible explanation? We test this directly. We formalize prospective explanation transportability: the extent to which an explanation learned from past data is recovered in a genuinely future context. This property is distinct from feature importance and measurement reliability. Using multi-year Genomes-to-Fields maize trials, where each new year is a real future context rather than a random resample, we evaluate 16 agronomically interpretable environmental concepts across five forward splits and one prospectively locked test year. Coarse environment-level explanations transport reliably (median cosine 0.83); their locked 2024 value, 0.811, fell within a prespecified 80% bootstrap percentile interval. For grain yield, genotype-conditioned explanations have a historical median cosine of 0.30 and range from 0.006 to 0.822 across years. Reliability does not order these outcomes: the year with the highest split-half reliability had the lowest explanation transport. Within-context resampling showed that GxE explanations were reproducible within a fixed context (0.77-0.95) while exhibiting lower prospective alignment across the 2020-2023 transitions (0.01-0.36). A second phenotype transports strongly at both resolutions, and non-temporal validation inflates apparent interaction-level transport by +0.22. These results show that target reliability and within-context explanation reproducibility do not by themselves establish when a model explanation can support a reproducible scientific claim.

</details>

Abstract: Rare language model behaviors can be difficult to study because obtaining them through ordinary sampling can require a very large number of samples. This motivates the behavior posterior sampling problem: given a language model and a behavior of interest, is there an algorithm that efficiently samples from the model's distribution over transcripts that exhibit this behavior? We introduce PosteriorBench, a benchmark for comparing algorithms by their compute cost and by how close their samples are to the true posterior in forward and reverse KL divergence. PosteriorBench contains 21 tasks on Qwen3.5-0.8B with behavior probabilities from $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-1}$, each with exact posterior samples from a corpus of one billion labeled responses. We find that no method we evaluate is both efficient and faithful: simple methods like prompting are cheap but plateau at a high KL divergence from the posterior, fine-tuning on posterior samples is often cost-prohibitive, and several training methods from prior work do not perform better. Building a posterior sampler that can cheaply approximate the true posterior remains an open problem, and we hope PosteriorBench can measure progress towards this objective. Code and data are available at https://github.com/TransluceAI/PosteriorBench.

We introduce CCGP-2M, a multimodal goal-preference dataset constructed from interaction trajectories in Craftax-Classic, and propose a vision-language model (VLM)-guided RL framework that learns environment-specific goal guidance from this dataset. In the offline stage, we align the VLM with goal preferences using Kullback-Leibler Token-level Direct Preference Optimization (KLTDPO), which combines token-level KL regularization with distributionally robust reweighting. Experiments on Craftax-Classic show that our method collects diamonds earlier than state-of-the-art baselines, achieves the highest score and reward, and attains the highest success rates on most achievements.

Abstract: Routing states in mixture-of-experts (MoE) models can distinguish correct from erroneous reasoning steps. If changing routing also changes a model's step-level verification judgment, it is tempting to conclude that the judgment follows what routing encodes. We test this inference with RouteVerify, a controlled intervention study that replaces selected internal activations from one solution trace with activations from another, while keeping the evaluated input unchanged. Across nine MoE checkpoints, we compare three intervention interfaces: router logits, MoE outputs, and post-MoE residual states. We vary the layers and token positions at which activations are replaced. We measure changes in a verification margin that quantifies the model's preference for INCORRECT over CORRECT. We also evaluate the same input with activations from correct versus erroneous steps to test whether the resulting judgment depends on the source step's correctness. We report four linked results. First, probes on router logits separate correct from erroneous steps in eight checkpoints (AUROC 0.80-0.88). Second, replacing router logits across all routed layers changes the judgment in all nine checkpoints, and by more than a norm-matched random replacement. Third, this change rarely depends on whether the source step is correct. At one layer, routing from erroneous steps and from correct steps produces indistinguishable margins in all nine checkpoints. Under all-layer routing, their difference is resolved and positive in only one. Fourth, the same difference is positive for local residual replacements in eight of nine checkpoints, so the measure does detect such dependence where it exists. Correctness is therefore readable from routing, and routing replacements move the judgment, but the movement rarely follows correctness.

</details>

Abstract: Organizations monitor their workers' activity for insider threats, in which workers misuse their legitimate access, by copying confidential files before leaving. Detectors score how unusual each day of activity is, flagging the most unusual for an analyst to review. Prominent current approaches use opaque neural networks, so explanations of a flag point to internal features: patterns of neuron activity most active on days of misuse. We audit such explanations for language-model detectors trained on CERT's simulated workplace logs. To test an explanation, we see what happens to the detector's score when we remove its features inside the model. We compare this to a control: a change that is as similar as possible to the removal, but without actually removing the features. Because defining "similar" requires choosing a metric, our central contribution is the tipping radius: a first-order prediction of how far that metric must move before the difference between removal and control disappears, a measure of how fragile the result is to that choice. In 33 of 34 runs of seven language models, the tipping radius is at most 18% of the distance between two standard choices of metric. Moving that far removes at least 58% of the difference or reverses it, while random moves of the same size leave it largely intact. The result replicates in thirteen of fourteen pre-declared primary runs on openly released models, though on our trained detectors the difference is often too small to measure. We also find that a flag's score partly reflects who the worker is, not what they did, and that the features named depend on whom they are compared against. Audits should therefore fix the control's metric in advance, report its tipping radius, and disclose that comparison.

</details>

Abstract: Low-rank plus sparse decompositions, such as robust PCA, typically model the low-rank component as dense and the sparse component as corruption. This viewpoint is poorly suited to graph adjacency matrices, where sparsity is structural and dense low-rank factors destroy the combinatorial and computational properties of the graph. We propose signed biclique decomposition (SBD), a sparse low-rank decomposition for integer-weighted graphs that represents the structured component as a sum of sparse signed binary rank-one factors, together with a sparse signed residual. The resulting representation remains discrete, interpretable, sparse, and compatible with standard linear algebra. We formulate fixed-rank and greedy SBD objectives and derive a gain-based greedy algorithm with theoretical guarantees. Empirically, SBD recovers block structure in noisy graphs, while its factorized representation enables efficient sparse matrix-matrix multiplication (SpMM). On real graphs with strong shared-neighborhood structure, SBD achieves compression factors above $10$ and SpMM speedups up to $6\times$ over cuSPARSE, suggesting a practical alternative to dense low-rank decompositions for graphs. Code is available at https://github.com/IAntoineV/sbd.

Abstract: Cross-environment deployment is the central obstacle to practical WiFi sensing: a pipeline reaching 0.89+ top-1 in its training environments collapses to 0.408 under Leave-One-Domain-Out (LODO) evaluation with only K-shot labels. A ceiling analysis shows the collapse is not an alignment problem: the action signal is strong across seen environments (about 0.89 K10), environment identity is decodable from the encoder (0.965), and the failure concentrates in the encoder's extrapolation to the novel environment. A pre-registered falsification chain confirms that "align the domains" methods--training-time alignment, readout-level prototype alignment, adversarial confusion, and covariance/subspace alignment (CORAL -3.8pp, subspace -8.3pp)--degrade performance, and that source-domain statistics anti-correlate with cross-environment contribution. The effective levers lie elsewhere; we combine them into Dec-H: (i) closed-form source within-class whitening, provably re-geometrizing the readout's L2 regularization (+8.6pp, zero cost); (ii) profile-mixing augmentation (+1.0pp); (iii) few-shot adaptation with alternating whitening refit (+1.5pp; fixed preconditioners are 20/20 negative); (iv) heterogeneous decision ensembles (+1.7pp); homogeneous scaling and source-weighted stacking are saturated or harmful. Dec-H reaches 0.560 K10 (0.812 K40), +15.2pp over the baseline, consistent across all four environments and seeds; all thirteen configurations were pre-registered with frozen criteria.

Abstract: Data-driven weather forecasting requires distinguishing local states and their temporal evolution. However, learned patterns remain implicit in neural forecasters, making their influence difficult to interpret. Failing to distinguish local evolution can lead to similar forecasts for locations that develop differently, increasing prediction error. This motivates explicit references for distinguishing and interpreting local patterns. Prototypes provide explicit pattern references, but sample- or task-level conditioning leaves open how to adapt to distinct locations and variable groups. To deal with this problem, we design ProtoCast, which uses group-specific prototype banks to adapt local features while accounting for cross-variable dependencies. Specifically, (1) we combine each group's historical features and temporal change with forecast context from cross-variable attention for evolution-aware prototype matching. (2) Sparse mixtures of prototype-specific low-rank operators tailor corrections to each group and location. A gated attention block then exchanges the corrected features across groups before prediction. Each group's correction decomposes into explicit prototype contributions before this fusion. (3) We jointly train the forecasting network and operators from scratch and refit each group's prototypes before every training epoch. Slot tracking preserves prototype-operator associations, making each prototype's contribution to local corrections more interpretable. Experiments on GlobalForecast and TyphoonTracking show improved forecasting accuracy, while prototype deletion links local feature contributions to changes in prediction error. This work advances the development of more accurate and interpretable data-driven weather forecasting models. Project page: https://zhixuanli.github.io/projects/li2026protocast

</details>

Abstract: We propose X Hedging, an alternative to the Deep Hedging framework that addresses computational and explainability limitations of neural-network-based hedging strategies. Deep Hedging leverages neural networks to learn flexible hedging policies but relies on opaque models and typically requires large training datasets, which can complicate interpretation and limit its effectiveness in data-constrained settings. X Hedging takes a step toward addressing these limitations by replacing neural networks with tree ensemble methods, specifically LightGBM, to estimate hedging strategies. This design provides a computationally efficient alternative while preserving strong hedging performance and moving toward more user-centered explainability. To integrate LightGBM into a reinforcement-learning-style hedging framework, we introduce an optimization approach that sequentially trains tree-ensemble models while introducing stochasticity through random training orders. In controlled experiments, X Hedging achieves performance comparable to Deep Hedging while offering greater user-centered explainability, improved computational efficiency, and substantially higher robustness when training data are limited. The numerical results demonstrate that tree-ensemble methods provide an effective computational alternative to neural networks for data-driven dynamic hedging.

</details>

Abstract: Lineage tracing links cell states through clonal ancestry, but does not generally identify unique source-descendant pairings. Learning from such data therefore requires a model of conditional outcomes, rather than only the terminal population distribution. We introduce LIFT, a latent flow framework for probabilistic cell-state generation from lineage-informed couplings. Variational inference encodes alternative outcomes in a latent space, while a conditional prior enables sampling from the initial state alone. A Mean Flow decoder predicts future cell state directly, requiring one decoder evaluation per sampling, which makes it convenient to predict cell fate through large scale Monte-Carlo sampling. This construction combines a representation of one-to-many relationships with efficient sampling and an analysis of conditional prediction error. Experiments on synthetic and real datasets demonstrate LIFT's ability to recover both population distribution and cell fate probabilities.

Abstract: Data-driven monitoring based on milling force can enable advanced manufacturing services including process optimization and condition monitoring. However, industrial implementation is often hindered by costly and intrusive hardware and large training data requirements. This paper therefore presents a hybrid approach using machine-internal signals and machining metadata to enable force-based monitoring on brownfield machines without additional sensors. Commonly available machine signals have relatively low sampling rates and cannot directly represent high-frequency milling-force variations, especially at higher spindle speeds. Instead of learning the complete force signal, which would increase model complexity, data requirements, and overfitting risk, a supervised machine-learning model predicts only the average milling force from selected machine-signal features and machining parameters. This low-frequency estimation parameterizes a static milling force model that reconstructs the force behavior over one spindle revolution. Although the static model does not reproduce all dynamic effects, it captures the force distribution, load levels, and angular regions of highest tool engagement. The approach is evaluated on one milling machine across varying process parameters, engagement conditions, cutting tools, and two workpiece materials. Dynamometer measurements are required only for development and validation. Ridge regression achieves an $R^2$ of $0.984$ and $0.991$ for average-force prediction on the test data ($\bar{F}_x$ and $\bar{F}_y$). The reconstructed force behavior over one spindle revolution achieves an $R^2$ of $0.962$ and $0.982$ ($F_x$ and $F_y$) compared with the reconstruction based on measured force features. The method provides a cost and computationally efficient basis for process monitoring, tool condition assessment, and process optimization on existing machines.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly need to process long documents and codebases, making long-context attention a major prefill bottleneck. Block sparse attention reduces computation by skipping unimportant blocks of the attention matrix, but scoring blocks accurately can itself be expensive. We propose Delta-Based Tile Scoring (DTS), a training-free, content-adaptive tile scoring method for sparse block attention. DTS selects query and key anchors according to local vector changes, using fewer representatives in redundant regions while retaining more detail in heterogeneous regions. To accelerate anchor selection, parallel delta packing partitions the query and key sequences into tile-aligned chunks and performs bounded local scans over these chunks concurrently. The selected tiles are then computed using the original query, key, and value vectors. On LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct, DTS keeps average RULER and LongBench scores within 0.10 percentage points of full attention, with RULER evaluated up to 128K tokens. At 128K tokens, DTS reduces measured scoring-pipeline latency by 38% compared with XAttention. Abstract: Interpretable models for tabular regression, such as the explainable boosting machine (EBM), restrict a prediction to main effects and pairwise interactions so that every part of it can be plotted. When the target depends on three or more inputs jointly, this restriction costs accuracy, and a black-box model with post hoc explanations becomes the usual fallback. Here we present MJBoost, a gradient boosting method whose weak learner is a single Kolmogorov-Arnold unit $g(\sum_p φ_p(x_p))$ with penalized spline inner and outer functions. Each unit is fitted in closed form by alternating penalized least squares, starting from principal Hessian directions of the residual, and categorical and missing inputs enter the units directly. Because a unit depends on each input only through one inner function, the fitted model has an exact functional ANOVA decomposition into main effects, pair effects and a higher-order remainder, which one-dimensional convolutions compute. On synthetic targets of interaction order three to six, the test error of MJBoost is up to 13 times lower than that of EBM, and its decomposition recovers the true variance share of every order to within 0.04. On the 35 regression tasks of the OpenML-CTR23 benchmark, it has a lower test RMSE than EBM on 21 tasks and a higher one on 2 (geometric-mean RMSE -29.1%). On these tasks the main and pair effects of the fitted model explain a median 0.992 of its variance, so the model reads as plots where the data allow, and the decomposition measures the higher-order part where they do not. With Newton steps the same algorithm classifies: on 32 binary tasks of OpenML-CC18 its test log-loss is on par with that of EBM, and on 11 multiclass tasks its mean log-loss is lower than that of EBM on 8 and than that of tuned LightGBM on 7.

Abstract: Salient Single-Positive Multi-Label Learning (SalSPML) assigns each image its most salient positive label, leaving other classes unlabeled. Existing methods construct class-level Salient Prototypes from observed positives to guide the recognition of unobserved classes. However, since salient annotations favor visually prominent instances, these prototypes may not fully capture the visual diversity of hidden positives, resulting in insufficient positive supervision. Abstract: Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) requires models to acquire new capabilities while retaining performance on previous tasks. A stability-oriented perspective common in conventional continual learning emphasizes knowledge preservation and interference mitigation. Complementing this perspective, we revisit CRL for vision-language models through policy improvement, a fundamental principle of RL, to preserve acquired performance gains. Using the performance difference lemma, we characterize historical performance changes through the current policy's expected advantages on historical task distributions, motivating replay of historical prompts with the current policy. To allocate limited replay memory, we propose Contrastive Likelihood Replay (CoLiR), which contrasts likelihood gains on correct responses from the final task policy with likelihood reductions on incorrect responses from the initial policy. Our analysis links correct-response likelihood gains to reference-policy advantages under an idealized KL-regularized optimal update and provides an endogenous-reward interpretation for general policy updates. CoLiR estimates scores from finite training rollouts and cached log-likelihoods, selects prompts at task boundaries, and replays them uniformly during subsequent training, with KL regularization restricted to replayed prompts. Memory construction only requires a small amount of additional cross-policy scoring but no additional response generation. Across three Qwen3-VL model sizes on MRCL, CoLiR achieves the highest mean final accuracy and mean task accuracy among the compared methods while maintaining competitive new-task performance.

Abstract: Few-step diffusion models are widely deployed for their efficiency. However, reinforcing them with black-box rewards, as is routine for multi-step models, faces two structural challenges. First, a few-step generator is a sampler rather than a score model, so its velocity is not the forward velocity of the generated distribution. Second, it must be anchored to the undistilled teacher, which shares no transitions with it. Existing few-step RL methods handle the latter with a marginal KL to the teacher, but each pays for the former in its reward credit. We propose Shadow Posterior Matching (SPM), which renoises generated samples into shadow inputs and obtains the credit from their shadow posterior. From the KL-regularized reward objective, we derive a regression loss that turns reward values into a reward direction while anchoring the generator's outputs to the teacher and aligning the shadow inputs with the deployed inputs. Applied to 4-step generators, SPM achieves higher rewards with less reward hacking than prior few-step RL methods.

Abstract: Global Workspace Theory (GWT), a leading theory of consciousness, holds that information becomes conscious when it is broadcast to specialized processes that use it. Building a workspace into a network does not reveal what the trained network computes with it: a module that reports an answer may have combined a broadcast message with its own knowledge, or merely forwarded an answer computed elsewhere, and task accuracy and ablation cannot tell these apart. We introduce a causal protocol that identifies who computes when modules communicate. Its central test transplants a message from another input, chosen so that ignoring, relaying and recombining it predict three different answers. On networks built to relay or to recombine, the test assigns the right computation in every case. Among trained workspace models it finds all three outcomes: one leaves its broadcast nearly unused, and of two models that differ only in their training data, one relays and the other recombines. In AFGW, a language model whose modules communicate only through a learned workspace, a transplanted message yields the answer that recombination predicts in 97% of cases, and its content acts by redirecting what the receiver retrieves from its own memory. The tests carry over to other communication designs and to a known interface of a pretrained Transformer, turning GWT's central claim into a testable property of trained models.

</details>

Abstract: Hybrid language models combine self-attention with recurrent layers whose in-place state updates restrict prefix reuse to exact saved positions. Saving states on a coarse grid wastes reusable prefixes, whereas existing fine-grained exact caches must cut the prefill forward pass at each mid-prompt checkpoint, adding scheduler steps that outweigh the reuse gains under load.

We present FrontierReplay, a prefix caching system that materializes exact checkpoints at these boundaries without cutting the prefill. While the forward pass runs uncut, FrontierReplay logs a short window of recurrent-layer inputs and invokes a cross-layer, state-only replay kernel to build the target state 6-37x cheaper per token than full recomputation without host synchronization. A frontier-aware retention policy prioritizes active anchors and proven branches while lending dead checkpoint slots to the attention KV cache.
  Across diverse hybrid architectures, model scales, and workloads, FrontierReplay delivers clear gains over existing exact caches, lowering median time to first token (TTFT) by 16-89% (up to 8.9x), raising 80% service-level objective (SLO) capacity by up to 1.73x, and improving goodput by up to 4.26x (3.67x at larger scales). Compared with approximate replay methods, FrontierReplay delivers up to 1.54x higher goodput while preserving exact task accuracy, avoiding the up to 17% accuracy drop suffered by approximation on long contexts.

Abstract: Fair machine learning typically evaluates the societal costs of a decision policy -- the loss incurred by the decision-maker (DM) and the unfair distribution of costs among demographic groups of decision-subjects (DS) -- on static data, where reducing one often comes at the expense of the other. Yet decisions change the populations on which they act, and prior work has shown that fairness interventions designed on static data may fail to benefit, or even harm, the disadvantaged group over time. We investigate a complementary question: When can a policy that is Pareto-optimal on pre-deployment data be outperformed by a fairer policy once each is evaluated on the population it induces, reducing costs for one stakeholder without increasing them for the other? In a setting where qualification scores evolve in response to binary decisions and their outcomes, we show that under Rawlsian fairness, which never lowers any group's acceptance rate, placing sufficiently high weight on fairness reduces societal costs whenever accepting disadvantaged DS at the DM's break-even point leaves them in a state less costly to the DM than rejecting them. Under egalitarian fairness, which may lower the advantaged group's acceptance rate, this additionally requires the reduction in DM loss on the disadvantaged group to outweigh the increase on the advantaged group. Under both fairness notions, however, when DM loss is accuracy-based, improvements in the post-deployment population may arise from declining DS qualification, revealing that gains for the DM do not necessarily translate into better outcomes for DS. Our results show that policy-induced population dynamics can overturn apparent performance-fairness trade-offs, allowing fairer policies to reduce the delayed societal costs of decision-making.

</details>

Abstract: Post-training low-rank compression of neural networks must not only approximate the original weights but also preserve the model's input-output behavior. Abstract: Auto-bidding is a core algorithmic component in online advertising auctions. In practical cold-start settings, iterative training is commonly used to compensate for low-quality, narrowly supported historical data. Unfortunately, in iterative training, representative diffusion-based AI-Generated Bidding (AIGB) methods fail to sustain extrapolation beyond the narrow data support and stagnate at a suboptimal level. In this paper, we theoretically attribute this stagnation to Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) collapse: the weak radial return signal is overwhelmed by a curvature-induced penalty. To break this stagnation, we propose \textbf{Iterative Scarcity-Guided Exploration (ISGE)}, which introduces a guidance handoff: as the return signal collapses, scarcity subsequently takes over as an exploratory signal to elevate the SNR above a critical threshold. Specifically, ISGE iteratively alternates between a \emph{Judger} that assigns scarcity scores to trajectories and an \emph{Explorer} that performs guided generation of high-scarcity and high-return trajectories, thereby bootstrapping from the narrow support with such self-generated trajectories. Extensive experiments on the industrial AuctionNet benchmark demonstrate that ISGE, starting from cold-start datasets, effectively surpasses the performance of the Full-Dataset baseline within three iterations. Code is available at https://github.com/alimama-tech/ISGE

</details>

Abstract: Comparing internal representations across trained models is important for identifying corresponding layers and selecting components for model reuse. Yet, direct comparisons at inference require evaluating candidate models on known reference inputs and collecting their internal activations. In this paper, we study whether internal representation similarity measured across independently trained models can instead be learned to predict in unseen models from their parameterized computations. We introduce a framework that applies a graph metanetwork for mapping each internal site to an embedding derived from its upstream computations while accounting for hidden-coordinate permutation and internal attention-basis transformations. We analyze the structural properties of our estimators and derive conditional guarantees for layer retrieval and stitching candidate selection. Empirically, we find that the learned predictors support relation estimation and low-regret layer retrieval. They also select useful stitching connections while not being trained on stitching outcomes. This opens up a new way to study representation similarity directly from trained neural networks: representational relations learned across a collection of trained models can generalize to new computations, and that the predicted relations can guide candidate selection before direct measurement or adapter fitting for functional evaluations.

Abstract: When a familiar rule stops working, an agent has to weigh what pre-training taught it against what it now observes. We ask how outcome-reward reinforcement learning (RL) changes this balance, in a puzzle world where both sides are known: small language models trained from scratch on games played by a Bayesian player, so that the prior and the right answer are exact. Base models hedge as their corpus did, and RL turns that hedging into decisions, in whichever direction the reward favours. But RL can only reinforce what it sees succeed. When the right action is rare, each run either learns it everywhere or not at all, and which runs take off is predicted before training by one count: the expected number of groups of attempts in which the action wins. About 35 such groups give even odds. The count holds for new and repeated problems and for groups of 4 to 256 attempts, and across learning rates, optimizers, GRPO variants and model sizes from 1.3M to 85M parameters, which move the threshold but not what is counted.

</details>

Abstract: Networks pass signals through many layers of interconnected units, so explaining what they compute means tracing how information travels from input to output and how pathways interact. Most existing approaches analyze only pairwise interactions between components such as units, layers or attention heads. Even when these measurements are combined post hoc into a circuit, they do not capture higher-order interactions along full causal chains (paths) from input to output, and so cannot address how exponentially many paths interact to construct network computation. This leaves a large set of mechanistic questions unanswerable, for example whether inhibitory pathways veto excitatory ones, and how the two combine to yield network decisions. To address this gap, we introduce Complete Path Contribution, a method to decompose the network output into mathematically complete contributions of individual paths to study path-level interactions directly and at scale. Partial summation then induces definitions of contributions for any circuit at any granularity level, with completeness guaranteed at every level. We further present an efficient algorithm that identifies the $k$ strongest paths without examining all of them, with runtime empirically linear in $k$ rather than exponential in depth. By applying Path Contribution to benchmark image classification networks, we quantify excitatory-inhibitory interactions and show how they collectively construct network decisions at a level of detail previously unmeasurable. Furthermore, in an interpretable model of the mouse retina, it reveals how dynamic receptive fields emerge from excitatory-inhibitory interaction motifs among pathways between different cell types in response to time-varying natural stimuli. Overall, Path Contribution reveals how a network's causal pathways interact at scale to transform input to output.

</details>

Abstract: Network topology influences decentralized federated learning through information mixing and the relative contribution of individual nodes. While existing analyses primarily focus on spectral mixing properties, they often assume doubly stochastic aggregation, overlooking topology-dependent influence under irregular communication networks. We develop a spectral framework that separates two complementary mechanisms: the dominant eigenmode determines the consensus trajectory and node influence, while non-dominant eigenmodes govern the propagation and attenuation of disagreement. Under uniform neighborhood averaging, we show that the stationary influence is proportional to node degree plus one and derive a bound relating model disagreement to spectral mixing and gradient heterogeneity. We then investigate how these mechanisms interact with the data distribution across nodes. Experiments across regular and heterogeneous network topologies reveal nearly identical learning dynamics under IID data, while network size and connectivity affect how unevenly distributed information is learned under non-IID data. For non-IID settings, we characterize how the distribution of data across nodes shapes the relative contribution of local information to consensus dynamics.
